<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">In this name that follows <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Slavic_naming_customs" title="Eastern Slavic naming customs">Eastern Slavic naming conventions</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Patronymic" title="Patronymic">patronymic</a> is <i> Alexandrovich</i>&#32;and the <a href="/wiki/Surname" title="Surname">family name</a> is <i> Romanov</i>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>

<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div data-file-height="1698" /></a></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;"><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a>, <a href="/wiki/Passion-Bearer" Russia</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">17 July</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Nicholas II</b> or <b>Nikolai II Alexandrovich Romanov</b><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> (18 May &#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 6 May&#93; 1868&#160;&#8211;&#32;17 July 1918), known in the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a> as <b>Saint Nicholas the Passion-Bearer</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> was the last <a href="/wiki/Emperor_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor of Russia">Emperor of Russia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Congress_Poland" title="Congress Poland">King of Congress Poland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_of_Finland" title="Grand Duke of Finland">Grand Duke of Finland</a>, ruling from 1 November 1894 until <a href="/wiki/Abdication_of_Nicholas_II" title="Abdication of Nicholas II">his abdication</a> on 15 March 1917. During his reign, Nicholas gave support to the economic and political reforms promoted by his prime ministers, <a href="/wiki/Sergei_Witte" title="Sergei Witte">Sergei Witte</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pyotr_Stolypin" title="Pyotr Stolypin">Pyotr Stolypin</a>. He advocated modernization based on foreign loans and close ties with France, but resisted giving the new parliament (the <a href="/wiki/State_Duma_(Russian_Empire)" title="State Duma (Russian Empire)">Duma</a>) major roles.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Longworth,_Phillip_2006_233_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longworth,_Phillip_2006_233-7">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, progress was undermined by Nicholas's commitment to <a href="/wiki/Autocracy" title="Autocracy">autocratic rule</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Longworth,_Phillip_2006_233_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Longworth,_Phillip_2006_233-7">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> strong aristocratic opposition and defeats sustained by the Russian military in the <a href="/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War" title="Russo-Japanese War">Russo-Japanese War</a> and <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Alexander_Rabinowitch_2008_1_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Alexander_Rabinowitch_2008_1-10">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Willmott,_H.P._2003_147_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Willmott,_H.P._2003_147-11">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> By March 1917, public support for Nicholas had collapsed and he was forced to abdicate the throne, thereby ending the <a href="/wiki/Romanov_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Romanov dynasty">Romanov dynasty</a>'s 304-year rule of Russia (1613–1917).
</p><p>Nicholas signed the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Russian_Convention" title="Anglo-Russian Convention">Anglo-Russian Convention</a> of 1907, which was designed to counter <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">Germany</a>'s attempts to gain influence in the Middle East; it ended the <a href="/wiki/Great_Game" title="Great Game">Great Game</a> of confrontation between Russia and the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>. He aimed to strengthen the <a href="/wiki/Franco-Russian_Alliance" title="Franco-Russian Alliance">Franco-Russian Alliance</a> and proposed the unsuccessful <a href="/wiki/Hague_Conventions_of_1899_and_1907" title="Hague Conventions of 1899 and 1907">Hague Convention of 1899</a> to promote disarmament and solve international disputes peacefully.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Domestically, he was criticised for his government's repression of political opponents and his perceived fault or inaction during the <a href="/wiki/Khodynka_Tragedy" title="Khodynka Tragedy">Khodynka Tragedy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pogroms_in_the_Russian_Empire" title="Pogroms in the Russian Empire">anti-Jewish pogroms</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bloody_Sunday_(1905)" title="Bloody Sunday (1905)">Bloody Sunday</a> and the violent suppression of the <a href="/wiki/1905_Russian_Revolution" title="1905 Russian Revolution">1905 Russian Revolution</a>. His popularity was further damaged by the Russo-Japanese War, which saw the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_Fleet" title="Baltic Fleet">Russian Baltic Fleet</a> annihilated at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tsushima" title="Battle of Tsushima">Battle of Tsushima</a>, together with the loss of Russian influence over Manchuria and Korea and the <a href="/wiki/Karafuto_Prefecture" title="Karafuto Prefecture">Japanese annexation of the south of Sakhalin Island</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/July_Crisis" title="July Crisis">July Crisis</a>, Nicholas supported <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia" title="Kingdom of Serbia">Serbia</a> and approved the mobilization of the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Russian_Army" title="Imperial Russian Army">Russian Army</a> on 30 July 1914. In response, Germany declared war on Russia on 1 August 1914 and its ally France on 3 August 1914,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> starting the <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">Great War</a>, later known as the First World War. The severe military losses led to a collapse of morale at the front and at home; a <a href="/wiki/General_strike" title="General strike">general strike</a> and a mutiny of the garrison in <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">Petrograd</a> sparked the <a href="/wiki/February_Revolution" title="February Revolution">February Revolution</a> and the disintegration of the monarchy's authority. After abdicating for himself and his son, Nicholas and his family were imprisoned by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government" title="Russian Provisional Government">Russian Provisional Government</a> and exiled to Siberia. After the <a href="/wiki/Bolsheviks" title="Bolsheviks">Bolsheviks</a> took power in the <a href="/wiki/October_Revolution" title="October Revolution">October Revolution</a>, the family was held in <a href="/wiki/Yekaterinburg" title="Yekaterinburg">Yekaterinburg</a>, where <a href="/wiki/Execution_of_the_Romanov_family" class="mw-redirect" title="Execution of the Romanov family">they were executed</a> on 17 July 1918.<sup id="cite_ref-Alexander_Rabinowitch_2008_1_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Alexander_Rabinowitch_2008_1-10">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Willmott,_H.P._2003_147_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Willmott,_H.P._2003_147-11">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1981, Nicholas, his wife, and their children were recognized as martyrs by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Orthodox Church Outside Russia">Russian Orthodox Church Outside Russia</a>, based in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Their gravesite was discovered in 1979, but this was not acknowledged until 1989. After the fall of the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>, the remains of the imperial family were exhumed, identified by DNA analysis, and re-interred with an elaborate state and church ceremony in St. Petersburg on 17 July 1998, exactly 80 years after their deaths. They were <a href="/wiki/Canonization_of_the_Romanovs" title="Canonization of the Romanovs">canonized</a> in 2000 by the Russian Orthodox Church as <a href="/wiki/Passion_bearer" title="Passion bearer">passion bearers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> In the years following his death, Nicholas was reviled by <a href="/wiki/Historiography_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Historiography in the Soviet Union">Soviet historians</a> and <a href="/wiki/Propaganda_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Propaganda in the Soviet Union">state propaganda</a> as a "callous tyrant" who "persecuted his own people while sending countless soldiers to their deaths in pointless conflicts".<sup id="cite_ref-Kallistov_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kallistov-17">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Despite being viewed more positively in recent years, the majority view among historians is that Nicholas was a well-intentioned yet poor ruler who proved incapable of handling the challenges facing his nation.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Esthus,_Raymond_A._1981_396–411_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Esthus,_Raymond_A._1981_396–411-19">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ferro,_Marc_1995_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ferro,_Marc_1995-20">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family_background">Birth and family background</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg/240px-Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="337" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg/360px-Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg/480px-Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1067" data-file-height="1500" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Maria_Fyodorovna_and_her_son_Niki.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II, <a href="/wiki/Breeching_(boys)" title="Breeching (boys)">unbreeched</a> at two years old, with his mother, Maria Feodorovna, in 1870</div></div></div>
<p>Grand Duke Nicholas was born on 18 May &#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 6 May&#93; 1868, in the <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Palace" title="Alexander Palace">Alexander Palace</a> in <a href="/wiki/Tsarskoye_Selo" title="Tsarskoye Selo">Tsarskoye Selo</a> south of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">Saint Petersburg</a>, during the reign of his grandfather <a href="/wiki/Alexander_II_of_Russia" title="Alexander II of Russia">Emperor Alexander II</a>. He was the eldest child of then-<a href="/wiki/Tsesarevich" title="Tsesarevich">Tsesarevich</a> <a href="/wiki/Alexander_III_of_Russia" title="Alexander III of Russia">Alexander Alexandrovich</a> and his wife, <a href="/wiki/Maria_Feodorovna_(Dagmar_of_Denmark)" title="Maria Feodorovna (Dagmar of Denmark)">Tsesarevna Maria Feodorovna</a> (née Princess Dagmar of Denmark). Grand Duke Nicholas' father was <a href="/wiki/Heir_apparent" title="Heir apparent">heir apparent</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian</a> throne as the second but eldest surviving son of Emperor Alexander II of Russia. His paternal grandparents were Emperor Alexander II and <a href="/wiki/Maria_Alexandrovna_(Marie_of_Hesse)" title="Maria Alexandrovna (Marie of Hesse)">Empress Maria Alexandrovna</a> (née Princess Marie of Hesse and by Rhine). His maternal grandparents were <a href="/wiki/Christian_IX_of_Denmark" title="Christian IX of Denmark">King Christian IX</a> and <a href="/wiki/Louise_of_Hesse-Kassel" title="Louise of Hesse-Kassel">Queen Louise of Denmark</a>.
</p><p>The young grand duke was christened in the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Chapel_of_Catherine_Palace&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chapel of Catherine Palace (page does not exist)">Chapel of the Resurrection</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Catherine_Palace" title="Catherine Palace">Catherine Palace</a> at <a href="/wiki/Tsarskoye_Selo" title="Tsarskoye Selo">Tsarskoye Selo</a> on 1 June &#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 20 May&#93; 1868 by the <a href="/wiki/Confessor" title="Confessor">confessor</a> of the imperial family, <a href="/wiki/Protopresbyter" class="mw-redirect" title="Protopresbyter">protopresbyter</a> Vasily Borisovich Bazhanov. His godparents were Emperor Alexander II (his paternal grandfather), <a href="/wiki/Louise_of_Hesse-Kassel" title="Louise of Hesse-Kassel">Queen Louise of Denmark</a> (his maternal grandmother), <a href="/wiki/Frederick_VIII_of_Denmark" title="Frederick VIII of Denmark">Crown Prince Frederick of Denmark</a> (his maternal uncle), and <a href="/wiki/Princess_Charlotte_of_W%C3%BCrttemberg" title="Princess Charlotte of Württemberg">Grand Duchess Elena Pavlovna</a> (his great great-aunt).<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The boy received the traditional <a href="/wiki/House_of_Romanov" title="House of Romanov">Romanov</a> name <i><a href="/wiki/Nicholas" title="Nicholas">Nicholas</a></i> and was named in memory of his father's older brother and mother's first <a href="/wiki/Fianc%C3%A9" class="mw-redirect" title="Fiancé">fiancé</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tsesarevich" title="Tsesarevich">Tsesarevich</a> <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Alexandrovich,_Tsesarevich_of_Russia" title="Nicholas Alexandrovich, Tsesarevich of Russia">Nicholas Alexandrovich of Russia</a>, who had died young in 1865.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Informally, he was known as "Nikki" throughout his life.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG/240px-Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG" decoding="async" width="240" height="339" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG/360px-Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG/480px-Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2128" data-file-height="3009" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Tsar_Nicholas_II_%26_King_George_V.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Emperor Nicholas II of Russia with his physically similar cousin, <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">King George V of the United Kingdom</a> (right), wearing German military uniforms in Berlin before the war; 1913</div></div></div>
<p>Nicholas was of primarily German and <a href="/wiki/Danes" title="Danes">Danish</a> descent, his last ethnically Russian ancestor being <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Anna_Petrovna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Anna Petrovna of Russia">Grand Duchess Anna Petrovna of Russia</a> (1708–1728), daughter of <a href="/wiki/Peter_the_Great" title="Peter the Great">Peter the Great</a>. On the other hand, Nicholas was related to several monarchs in Europe. His mother's siblings included Kings <a href="/wiki/Frederick_VIII_of_Denmark" title="Frederick VIII of Denmark">Frederick VIII of Denmark</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_I_of_Greece" title="George I of Greece">George I of Greece</a>, as well as the United Kingdom's <a href="/wiki/Queen_Alexandra" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen Alexandra">Queen Alexandra</a> (consort of <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">King Edward VII</a>). Nicholas, his wife Alexandra, and <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_II,_German_Emperor" title="Wilhelm II, German Emperor">German emperor Wilhelm II</a> were all first cousins of <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">King George V of the United Kingdom</a>. Nicholas was also a first cousin of both <a href="/wiki/Haakon_VII_of_Norway" title="Haakon VII of Norway">King Haakon VII</a> and <a href="/wiki/Maud_of_Wales" title="Maud of Wales">Queen Maud of Norway</a>, as well as <a href="/wiki/Christian_X_of_Denmark" title="Christian X of Denmark">King Christian X of Denmark</a> and <a href="/wiki/Constantine_I_of_Greece" title="Constantine I of Greece">King Constantine I of Greece</a>. Nicholas and Wilhelm II were in turn second cousins once-removed, as each descended from <a href="/wiki/Frederick_William_III_of_Prussia" title="Frederick William III of Prussia">King Frederick William III of Prussia</a>, as well as third cousins, as they were both great-great-grandsons of <a href="/wiki/Tsar_Paul_I_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Tsar Paul I of Russia">Tsar Paul I of Russia</a>. In addition to being second cousins through descent from <a href="/wiki/Louis_II,_Grand_Duke_of_Hesse" title="Louis II, Grand Duke of Hesse">Louis II, Grand Duke of Hesse</a> and his wife <a href="/wiki/Princess_Wilhelmine_of_Baden" title="Princess Wilhelmine of Baden">Princess Wilhelmine of Baden</a>, Nicholas and Alexandra were also third cousins once-removed, as they were both descendants of <a href="/wiki/Frederick_William_II_of_Prussia" title="Frederick William II of Prussia">King Frederick William II of Prussia</a>.
</p><p>Tsar Nicholas II was the first cousin once-removed of <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Nikolai_Nikolaevich_of_Russia_(1856%E2%80%931929)" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Duke Nikolai Nikolaevich of Russia (1856–1929)">Grand Duke Nicholas Nikolaevich</a>. To distinguish between them, the Grand Duke was often known within the imperial family as "Nikolasha" and "Nicholas the Tall", while the Tsar was "Nicholas the Short".
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood">Childhood</span></h3>
<p>Grand Duke Nicholas was to have five younger siblings: <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Alexander_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Alexander Alexandrovich of Russia">Alexander</a> (1869–1870), <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_George_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke George Alexandrovich of Russia">George</a> (1871–1899), <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Xenia_Alexandrovna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Xenia Alexandrovna of Russia">Xenia</a> (1875–1960), <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Michael_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Michael Alexandrovich of Russia">Michael</a> (1878–1918) and <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Olga_Alexandrovna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Olga Alexandrovna of Russia">Olga</a> (1882–1960). Nicholas often referred to his father nostalgically in letters after Alexander's death in 1894. He was also very close to his mother, as revealed in their published letters to each other.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
In his childhood, Nicholas, his parents and siblings made annual visits to the Danish royal palaces of <a href="/wiki/Fredensborg_Palace" title="Fredensborg Palace">Fredensborg</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bernstorff_Palace" title="Bernstorff Palace">Bernstorff</a> to visit his grandparents, the king and queen. The visits also served as family reunions, as his mother's siblings would also come from the United Kingdom, Germany and Greece with their respective families.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> It was there in 1883, that he had a flirtation with one of his British first cousins, <a href="/wiki/Princess_Victoria_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Princess Victoria of the United Kingdom">Princess Victoria</a>. In 1873, Nicholas also accompanied his parents and younger brother, two-year-old George, on a two-month, semi-official visit to the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> In London, Nicholas and his family stayed at <a href="/wiki/Marlborough_House" title="Marlborough House">Marlborough House</a>, as guests of his "Uncle Bertie" and "Aunt Alix", the Prince and Princess of Wales, where he was spoiled by his uncle.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Tsesarevich">Tsesarevich</span></h2>
<p>On 1 March 1881,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> following <a href="/wiki/Assassination_of_Alexander_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Assassination of Alexander II">the assassination</a> of his grandfather, Tsar Alexander II, Nicholas became heir apparent upon his father's accession as Alexander III. Nicholas and his other family members bore witness to Alexander II's death, having been present at the <a href="/wiki/Winter_Palace" title="Winter Palace">Winter Palace</a> in Saint Petersburg, where he was brought after the attack.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> For security reasons, the new Tsar and his family relocated their primary residence to the <a href="/wiki/Gatchina_Palace" title="Gatchina Palace">Gatchina Palace</a> outside the city, only entering the capital for various ceremonial functions. On such occasions, Alexander III and his family occupied the nearby <a href="/wiki/Anichkov_Palace" title="Anichkov Palace">Anichkov Palace</a>.
</p><p>In 1884, Nicholas's coming-of-age ceremony was held at the Winter Palace, where he pledged his loyalty to his father. Later that year, Nicholas's uncle, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Sergei_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Sergei Alexandrovich of Russia">Grand Duke Sergei Alexandrovich</a>, married <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Elizabeth_Fyodorovna" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Duchess Elizabeth Fyodorovna">Princess Elizabeth</a>, daughter of <a href="/wiki/Louis_IV,_Grand_Duke_of_Hesse" title="Louis IV, Grand Duke of Hesse">Louis IV, Grand Duke of Hesse</a> and his late wife <a href="/wiki/Princess_Alice_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Princess Alice of the United Kingdom">Princess Alice of the United Kingdom</a> (who had died in 1878), and a granddaughter of <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a>. At the wedding in St. Petersburg, the sixteen-year-old Tsesarevich met with and admired the bride's youngest surviving sister, twelve-year-old <a href="/wiki/Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)" title="Alexandra Feodorovna (Alix of Hesse)">Princess Alix</a>. Those feelings of admiration blossomed into love following her visit to St. Petersburg five years later in 1889. Alix had feelings for him in turn. As a devout Lutheran, she was initially reluctant to convert to Russian Orthodoxy to marry Nicholas, but later relented.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas1868.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Nicholas1868.jpg/170px-Nicholas1868.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Nicholas1868.jpg/255px-Nicholas1868.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Nicholas1868.jpg/340px-Nicholas1868.jpg 2x" data-file-width="522" data-file-height="676" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas1868.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas Alexandrovich, <a href="/wiki/Tsarevich" title="Tsarevich">Tsarevich</a> of <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russia</a>, 1880s</div></div></div>
<p>In 1890 Nicholas, his younger brother George, and their cousin <a href="/wiki/Prince_George_of_Greece_and_Denmark" title="Prince George of Greece and Denmark">Prince George of Greece</a>, set out on a <a href="/wiki/Eastern_journey_of_Nicholas_II" title="Eastern journey of Nicholas II">world tour</a>, although Grand Duke George fell ill and was sent home partway through the trip. Nicholas visited Egypt, India, Singapore, and Siam (Thailand), receiving honors as a distinguished guest in each country. During his trip through Japan, Nicholas had a large <a href="/wiki/Japanese_dragon" title="Japanese dragon">dragon</a> tattooed on his right forearm by Japanese tattoo artist Hori Chyo.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> His cousin George V of the United Kingdom had also received a dragon tattoo from Hori in <a href="/wiki/Yokohama" title="Yokohama">Yokohama</a> years before. It was during his visit to <a href="/wiki/Otsu" class="mw-redirect" title="Otsu">Otsu</a>, that <a href="/wiki/Tsuda_Sanz%C5%8D" title="Tsuda Sanzō">Tsuda Sanzō</a>, one of his escorting policemen, swung at the Tsesarevich's face with a sabre, an event known as the <a href="/wiki/%C5%8Ctsu_incident" title="Ōtsu incident">Ōtsu incident</a>. Nicholas was left with a 9 centimeter long scar on the right side of his forehead, but his wound was not life-threatening. The incident cut his trip short.<sup id="cite_ref-kowner_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kowner-32">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Returning overland to St. Petersburg, he was present at the ceremonies in <a href="/wiki/Vladivostok" title="Vladivostok">Vladivostok</a> commemorating the beginning of work on the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Siberian_Railway" title="Trans-Siberian Railway">Trans-Siberian Railway</a>. In 1893, Nicholas traveled to London on behalf of his parents to be present at the wedding of his cousin <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">the Duke of York</a> to <a href="/wiki/Princess_Mary_of_Teck" class="mw-redirect" title="Princess Mary of Teck">Princess Mary of Teck</a>. Queen Victoria was struck by the physical resemblance between the two cousins, and their appearances confused some at the wedding. During this time, Nicholas had an affair with St. Petersburg ballerina <a href="/wiki/Mathilde_Kschessinska" title="Mathilde Kschessinska">Mathilde Kschessinska</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though Nicholas was heir-apparent to the throne, his father failed to prepare him for his future role as Tsar. He attended meetings of the <a href="/wiki/State_Council_(Russian_Empire)" title="State Council (Russian Empire)">State Council</a>; however, as his father was only in his forties, it was expected that it would be many years before Nicholas succeeded to the throne.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Sergei_Witte" title="Sergei Witte">Sergei Witte</a>, Russia's finance minister, saw things differently and suggested to the Tsar that Nicholas be appointed to the Siberian Railway Committee.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Alexander argued that Nicholas was not mature enough to take on serious responsibilities, having once stated "Nikki is a good boy, but he has a poet's soul...God help him!" Witte stated that if Nicholas was not introduced to state affairs, he would never be ready to understand them.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Alexander's assumptions that he would live a long life and had years to prepare Nicholas for becoming Tsar proved wrong, as by 1894, Alexander's health was failing.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Engagement">Engagement</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Wedding_of_Nicholas_II_and_Alexandra_Feodorovna" title="Wedding of Nicholas II and Alexandra Feodorovna">Wedding of Nicholas II and Alexandra Feodorovna</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg/170px-Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="232" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg/255px-Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg/340px-Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="716" data-file-height="976" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Engagement_official_picture_of_Alexandra_and_Nicholas.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Official engagement photograph of Nicholas II and Alexandra, April 1894</div></div></div>
<p>In April 1894, Nicholas joined his Uncle Sergei and Aunt Elizabeth on a journey to Coburg, Germany, for the wedding of Elizabeth's and Alix's brother, <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Louis,_Grand_Duke_of_Hesse" title="Ernest Louis, Grand Duke of Hesse">Ernest Louis, Grand Duke of Hesse</a>, to their mutual first cousin <a href="/wiki/Princess_Victoria_Melita_of_Saxe-Coburg_and_Gotha" title="Princess Victoria Melita of Saxe-Coburg and Gotha">Princess Victoria Melita of Saxe-Coburg and Gotha</a>. Other guests included Queen Victoria, Kaiser Wilhelm II, the <a href="/wiki/Victoria,_Princess_Royal" title="Victoria, Princess Royal">Empress Frederick</a> (Kaiser Wilhelm's mother and Queen Victoria's eldest daughter), Nicholas's uncle, the <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">Prince of Wales</a>, and the bride's parents, <a href="/wiki/Prince_Alfred,_Duke_of_Edinburgh" class="mw-redirect" title="Prince Alfred, Duke of Edinburgh">the Duke</a> and <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Maria_Alexandrovna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Maria Alexandrovna of Russia">Duchess of Saxe-Coburg and Gotha</a>.
</p><p>Once in Coburg Nicholas proposed to Alix, but she rejected his proposal, being reluctant to convert to Orthodoxy. But the Kaiser later informed her she had a duty to marry Nicholas and to convert, as her sister Elizabeth had done in 1892. Thus once she changed her mind, Nicholas and Alix became officially engaged on 20 April 1894. Nicholas's parents initially hesitated to give the engagement their blessing, as Alix had made poor impressions during her visits to Russia. They gave their consent only when they saw Tsar Alexander's health deteriorating.
</p><p>That summer, Nicholas travelled to England to visit both Alix and the Queen. The visit coincided with the birth of <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">the Duke</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Teck" title="Mary of Teck">Duchess of York</a>'s first child, the future <a href="/wiki/King_Edward_VIII" class="mw-redirect" title="King Edward VIII">King Edward VIII</a>. Along with being present at the christening, Nicholas and Alix were listed among the child's godparents.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> After several weeks in England, Nicholas returned home for the wedding of his sister, Xenia, to a cousin, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Alexander_Mikhailovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Alexander Mikhailovich of Russia">Grand Duke Alexander Mikhailovich</a> ("Sandro").<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:1904-1905peterhof.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/1904-1905peterhof.jpg/220px-1904-1905peterhof.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="282" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/1904-1905peterhof.jpg/330px-1904-1905peterhof.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/1904-1905peterhof.jpg/440px-1904-1905peterhof.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2458" data-file-height="3156" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:1904-1905peterhof.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II and family in 1904</div></div></div>
<p>By that autumn, Alexander III lay dying. Upon learning that he would live only a fortnight, the Tsar had Nicholas summon Alix to the imperial palace at <a href="/wiki/Livadia_Palace" title="Livadia Palace">Livadia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Alix arrived on 22 October; the Tsar insisted on receiving her in full uniform. From his deathbed, he told his son to heed the advice of Witte, his most capable minister. Ten days later, Alexander III died at the age of forty-nine, leaving twenty-six-year-old Nicholas as Emperor of Russia. That evening, Nicholas was consecrated by his father's priest as Tsar Nicholas II and, the following day, Alix was received into the Russian Orthodox Church, taking the name Alexandra Feodorovna with the title of <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Duchess">Grand Duchess</a> and the style of <i><a href="/wiki/Imperial_Highness" title="Imperial Highness">Imperial Highness</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Accession.2C_reign_and_marriage"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Accession,_reign_and_marriage">Accession, reign and marriage</span></h2>
<p>Nicholas may have felt unprepared for the duties of the crown, for he asked his cousin and brother-in-law, Grand Duke Alexander,<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> "What is going to happen to me and all of Russia?"<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Though perhaps under-prepared and unskilled, Nicholas was not altogether untrained for his duties as Tsar. Nicholas chose to maintain the conservative policies favoured by his father throughout his reign. While Alexander III had concentrated on the formulation of general policy, Nicholas devoted much more attention to the details of administration.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG/170px-Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="281" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG/255px-Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG/340px-Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG 2x" data-file-width="784" data-file-height="1296" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Czar_Nicholas_Alexandra_Olga.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Emperor Nicholas II and Empress Alexandra with their first child, Grand Duchess Olga, 1896</div></div></div>
<p>Leaving Livadia on 7 November, Tsar Alexander's funeral procession—which included Nicholas's maternal aunt through marriage and paternal first cousin once removed <a href="/wiki/Olga_Constantinovna_of_Russia" title="Olga Constantinovna of Russia">Queen Olga of Greece</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">Prince</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alexandra_of_Denmark" title="Alexandra of Denmark">Princess of Wales</a>—arrived in Moscow. After lying in state in the Kremlin, the body of the Tsar was taken to St. Petersburg, where the funeral was held on 19 November.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicholas and Alix's wedding was originally scheduled for the spring of 1895, but it was moved forward at Nicholas's insistence. Staggering under the weight of his new office, he had no intention of allowing the one person who gave him confidence to leave his side.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Instead, <a href="/wiki/Wedding_of_Nicholas_II_and_Alexandra_Feodorovna" title="Wedding of Nicholas II and Alexandra Feodorovna">Nicholas's wedding to Alix</a> took place on 26 November 1894, which was the birthday of the Dowager Empress Marie Feodorovna, and court mourning could be slightly relaxed. Alexandra wore the traditional dress of Romanov brides, and Nicholas a <a href="/wiki/Hussar" title="Hussar">hussar</a>'s uniform. Nicholas and Alexandra, each holding a lit candle, faced the palace priest and were married a few minutes before one in the afternoon.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502,_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502%2C_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg/220px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502%2C_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502%2C_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg/330px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502%2C_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502%2C_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg/440px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502%2C_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="520" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-1991-1101-502,_Russische_Zarenfamilie_bei_Bootsausflug.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas (<i>left</i>) and his family on a boat trip in the Finnish archipelago in 1909</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Coronation">Coronation</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg/220px-Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg/330px-Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg/440px-Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="2684" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Walentin_Serow_Kroenung_Zar_Nikolai_II_anagoria.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Coronation of Nicholas II by <a href="/wiki/Valentin_Serov" title="Valentin Serov">Valentin Serov</a></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_Nicholas_II_and_Alexandra_Feodorovna" title="Coronation of Nicholas II and Alexandra Feodorovna">Coronation of Nicholas II and Alexandra Feodorovna</a></div>
<p>Despite a visit to the United Kingdom in 1893, where he observed the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> in debate and was seemingly impressed by the machinery of <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy" title="Constitutional monarchy">constitutional monarchy</a>, Nicholas turned his back on any notion of giving away any power to elected representatives in Russia. Shortly after he came to the <a href="/wiki/Throne" title="Throne">throne</a>, a deputation of peasants and workers from various towns' local assemblies (<i><a href="/wiki/Zemstvos" class="mw-redirect" title="Zemstvos">zemstvos</a></i>) came to the Winter Palace proposing court reforms, such as the adoption of a constitutional monarchy,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> and reform that would improve the political and economic life of the peasantry, in the <a href="/wiki/Tver_Address" title="Tver Address">Tver Address</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although the addresses they had sent in beforehand were couched in mild and loyal terms, Nicholas was angry and ignored advice from an Imperial Family Council by saying to them:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>... it has come to my knowledge that during the last months there have been heard in some assemblies of the zemstvos the voices of those who have indulged in a senseless dream that the zemstvos be called upon to participate in the government of the country. I want everyone to know that I will devote all my strength to maintain, for the good of the whole nation, the principle of absolute <a href="/wiki/Autocracy" title="Autocracy">autocracy</a>, as firmly and as strongly as did my late lamented father.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>On 26 May 1896, Nicholas's formal <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_the_Russian_monarch" title="Coronation of the Russian monarch">coronation</a> as Tsar was held in <a href="/wiki/Dormition_Cathedral,_Moscow" title="Dormition Cathedral, Moscow">Uspensky Cathedral</a> located within the <a href="/wiki/Kremlin" title="Kremlin">Kremlin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-51">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg/220px-Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="375" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg/330px-Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg/440px-Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1141" data-file-height="1944" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_of_Russia_1892.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas as Tsesarevich in 1892</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Khodynka_Tragedy" title="Khodynka Tragedy">Khodynka Tragedy</a></div>
<p>In a celebration on 27 May 1896, a large festival with food, free beer and souvenir cups was held in <a href="/wiki/Khodynka_Field" title="Khodynka Field">Khodynka Field</a> outside Moscow. Khodynka was chosen as the location as it was the only place near Moscow large enough to hold all of the Moscow citizens.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Khodynka was primarily used as a military training ground and the field was uneven with trenches. Before the food and drink was handed out, rumours spread that there would not be enough for everyone. As a result, the crowd rushed to get their share and individuals were tripped and trampled upon, suffocating in the dirt of the field.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Of the approximate 100,000 in attendance, it is estimated that 1,389 individuals died<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-51">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> and roughly 1,300 were injured.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The Khodynka Tragedy was seen as an ill omen and Nicholas found gaining popular trust difficult from the beginning of his reign. The French ambassador's gala was planned for that night. The Tsar wanted to stay in his chambers and pray for the lives lost, but his uncles believed that his absence at the ball would strain relations with France, particularly the 1894 <a href="/wiki/Franco-Russian_Alliance" title="Franco-Russian Alliance">Franco-Russian Alliance</a>. Thus Nicholas attended the party; as a result the mourning populace saw Nicholas as frivolous and uncaring.
</p><p>During the autumn after the coronation, Nicholas and Alexandra made a tour of Europe. After making visits to the emperor and empress of Austria-Hungary, the Kaiser of Germany, and Nicholas's Danish grandparents and relatives, Nicholas and Alexandra took possession of their new yacht, the <i><a href="/wiki/Russian_yacht_Standart" title="Russian yacht Standart">Standart</a>, </i>which had been built in Denmark.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> From there, they made a journey to Scotland to spend some time with Queen Victoria at <a href="/wiki/Balmoral_Castle" title="Balmoral Castle">Balmoral Castle</a>. While Alexandra enjoyed her reunion with her grandmother, Nicholas complained in a letter to his mother about being forced to go shooting with his uncle, the Prince of Wales, in bad weather, and was suffering from a bad toothache.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first years of his reign saw little more than continuation and development of the policy pursued by Alexander III. Nicholas allotted money for the <a href="/wiki/All-Russia_exhibition_1896" class="mw-redirect" title="All-Russia exhibition 1896">All-Russia exhibition of 1896</a>. In 1897 restoration of <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold standard</a> by Sergei Witte, Minister of Finance, completed the series of financial reforms, initiated fifteen years earlier. By 1902 the Trans-Siberian Railway was nearing completion; this helped the Russians trade in the Far East but the railway still required huge amounts of work.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg/180px-Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="180" height="272" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg/270px-Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg/360px-Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="257" data-file-height="389" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Imperial_Monogram_of_Tsar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Imperial monogram</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecclesiastical_affairs">Ecclesiastical affairs</span></h3>
<p>Nicholas always believed God chose him to be the tsar and therefore the decisions of the tsar reflected the will of God and could not be disputed. He was convinced that the simple people of Russia understood this and loved him, as demonstrated by the display of affection he perceived when he made public appearances. His old-fashioned belief made for a very stubborn ruler who rejected constitutional limitations on his power. It put the tsar at variance with the emerging political consensus among the Russian elite. It was further belied by the subordinate position of the Church in the bureaucracy. The result was a new distrust between the tsar and the church hierarchy and between those hierarchs and the people. Thereby the tsar's base of support was conflicted.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1903, Nicholas threw himself into an ecclesiastical crisis regarding the <a href="/wiki/Canonisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonisation">canonisation</a> of <a href="/wiki/Seraphim_of_Sarov" title="Seraphim of Sarov">Seraphim of Sarov</a>. The previous year, it had been suggested that if he were canonised, the imperial couple would beget a son and heir to throne. While Alexandra demanded in July 1902 that Seraphim be canonised in less than a week, Nicholas demanded that he be canonised within a year. Despite a public outcry, the Church bowed to the intense imperial pressure, declaring Seraphim worthy of canonisation in January 1903. That summer, the imperial family travelled to Sarov for the canonisation.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Initiatives_in_foreign_affairs">Initiatives in foreign affairs</span></h3>
<p>According to his biographer:
</p>
<dl><dd>His tolerance if not preference for charlatans and adventurers extended to grave matters of external policy, and his vacillating conduct and erratic decisions aroused misgivings and occasional alarm among his more conventional advisers. The foreign ministry itself was not a bastion of diplomatic expertise. Patronage and "connections" were the keys to appointment and promotion.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<p>Emperor <a href="/wiki/Franz_Joseph_I_of_Austria" title="Franz Joseph I of Austria">Franz Joseph</a> of Austria-Hungary paid a state visit in April 1897 that was a success. It produced a "gentlemen's agreement" to keep the status quo in the Balkans, and a somewhat similar commitment became applicable to Constantinople and the Straits. The result was years of peace that allowed for rapid economic growth.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG/220px-Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG/330px-Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG/440px-Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2481" data-file-height="1647" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Faure_Loubet_Carnot_Alexandre_Nicolas_Grandes_manoeuvres_de_l%27est_1901_carte_postale.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Souvenir postcard of the <a href="/wiki/French_maneuvers_of_1901" title="French maneuvers of 1901">French maneuvers of 1901</a> attended by Nicholas II and Alexandra</div></div></div>
<p>Nicholas followed the policies of his father, strengthening the Franco-Russian Alliance and pursuing a policy of general European pacification, which culminated in the famous <a href="/wiki/Hague_Conventions_(1899_and_1907)" class="mw-redirect" title="Hague Conventions (1899 and 1907)">Hague peace conference</a>. This conference, suggested and promoted by Nicholas II, was convened with the view of terminating the <a href="/wiki/Arms_race" title="Arms race">arms race</a>, and setting up machinery for the peaceful settlement of international disputes. The results of the conference were less than expected due to the mutual distrust existing between great powers. Nevertheless, the Hague conventions were among the first formal statements of the laws of war.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas II became the hero of the dedicated disciples of peace. In 1901 he and the Russian diplomat <a href="/wiki/Friedrich_Martens" title="Friedrich Martens">Friedrich Martens</a> were nominated for the <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a> for the initiative to convene the Hague Peace Conference and contributing to its implementation.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> However historian Dan L. Morrill states that "most scholars" agree that the invitation was "conceived in fear, brought forth in deceit, and swaddled in humanitarian ideals...Not from humanitarianism, not from love for mankind."<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Russo-Japanese_War">Russo-Japanese War</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War" title="Russo-Japanese War">Russo-Japanese War</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Oleg-cruiser.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Oleg-cruiser.jpg/220px-Oleg-cruiser.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="156" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Oleg-cruiser.jpg/330px-Oleg-cruiser.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Oleg-cruiser.jpg/440px-Oleg-cruiser.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1897" data-file-height="1342" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Oleg-cruiser.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Russian <a href="/wiki/Baltic_Fleet" title="Baltic Fleet">Baltic Fleet</a> was annihilated by the Japanese at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tsushima" title="Battle of Tsushima">Battle of Tsushima</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>A clash between Russia and the <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Empire of Japan</a> was almost inevitable by the turn of the 20th century. Russia had expanded in the Far East, and the growth of its settlement and territorial ambitions, as its southward path to the <a href="/wiki/Balkans" title="Balkans">Balkans</a> was frustrated, conflicted with Japan's own territorial ambitions on the Asian mainland. Nicholas pursued an aggressive foreign policy with regards to <a href="/wiki/Manchuria" title="Manchuria">Manchuria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Korea" title="Korea">Korea</a>, and strongly supported the scheme for timber concessions in these areas as developed by the <a href="/wiki/Aleksandr_Mikhailovich_Bezobrazov" title="Aleksandr Mikhailovich Bezobrazov">Bezobrazov group</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kowner_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kowner-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-online_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-online-65">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before the war in 1901, Nicholas told <a href="/wiki/Prince_Henry_of_Prussia_(1862%E2%80%931929)" title="Prince Henry of Prussia (1862–1929)">Prince Henry of Prussia</a> "I do not want to seize <a href="/wiki/Korean_Empire" title="Korean Empire">Korea</a> but under no circumstances can I allow <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Japan</a> to become firmly established there. That will be a casus belli."<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>War began in February 1904 with a preemptive Japanese attack on the Russian fleet in <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Port_Arthur" title="Battle of Port Arthur">Port Arthur</a>, prior to a formal declaration of war.<sup id="cite_ref-Kowner_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kowner-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the Russian Far East fleet trapped at Port Arthur, the only other Russian Fleet was the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_Fleet" title="Baltic Fleet">Baltic Fleet</a>; it was half a world away, but the decision was made to send the fleet on a nine-month voyage to the East. The United Kingdom would not allow the Russian navy to use the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a>, due to <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Japanese_Alliance" title="Anglo-Japanese Alliance">its alliance</a> with the Empire of Japan, and due to the <a href="/wiki/Dogger_Bank_incident" title="Dogger Bank incident">Dogger Bank incident</a> where the Baltic Fleet mistakenly fired on British fishing boats in the North Sea. The Baltic Fleet traversed the world to lift the blockade on Port Arthur, but after many misadventures on the way, was nearly annihilated by the Japanese in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tsushima" title="Battle of Tsushima">Battle of the Tsushima Strait</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kowner_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kowner-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> On land the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Russian_Army" title="Imperial Russian Army">Imperial Russian Army</a> experienced logistical problems. While commands and supplies came from <a href="/wiki/St._Petersburg" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Petersburg">St. Petersburg</a>, combat took place in east Asian ports with only the Trans-Siberian Railway for transport of supplies as well as troops both ways.<sup id="cite_ref-Kowner_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kowner-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The 9,200-kilometre (5,700&#160;mi) rail line between St. Petersburg and Port Arthur was single-track, with no track around <a href="/wiki/Lake_Baikal" title="Lake Baikal">Lake Baikal</a>, allowing only gradual build-up of the forces on the front. <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Port_Arthur" title="Siege of Port Arthur">Besieged Port Arthur</a> fell to the Japanese, after nine months of resistance.<sup id="cite_ref-Kowner_64-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kowner-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Russia faced imminent defeat by the Japanese, the call for peace grew. Nicholas's mother, as well as his cousin Emperor Wilhelm II, urged Nicholas to negotiate for peace. Despite the efforts, Nicholas remained evasive, sending a telegram to the Kaiser on 10 October that it was his intent to keep on fighting until the Japanese were driven from Manchuria.<sup id="cite_ref-Kowner_64-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kowner-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> It was not until 27–28 May 1905 and the annihilation of the Russian fleet by the Japanese, that Nicholas finally decided to sue for peace.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas II accepted American mediation, appointing Sergei Witte chief plenipotentiary for the peace talks. The war was ended by the signing of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Portsmouth" title="Treaty of Portsmouth">Treaty of Portsmouth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kowner_64-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kowner-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Tsar.27s_confidence_in_victory"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Tsar's_confidence_in_victory">Tsar's confidence in victory</span></h4>
<p>Nicholas's stance on the war was so at variance with the obvious facts that many observers were baffled. He saw the war as an easy God-given victory that would raise Russian morale and patriotism. He ignored the financial repercussions of a long-distance war.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Rotem Kowner argues that during his visit to Japan in 1891, where Nicholas was <a href="/wiki/%C5%8Ctsu_incident" title="Ōtsu incident">attacked</a> by a Japanese policeman, he regarded the Japanese as small of stature, feminine, weak, and inferior. He ignored reports of the prowess of Japanese soldiers in the Sino-Japanese War (1894–95) and reports on the capabilities of the Japanese fleet, as well as negative reports on the lack of readiness of Russian forces.<sup id="cite_ref-kowner_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kowner-32">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before the Japanese attack on Port Arthur, Nicholas held firm to the belief that there would be no war. Despite the onset of the war and the many defeats Russia suffered, Nicholas still believed in, and expected, a final victory, maintaining an image of the racial inferiority and military weakness of the Japanese.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Throughout the war, the tsar demonstrated total confidence in Russia's ultimate triumph. His advisors never gave him a clear picture of Russia's weaknesses. Despite the continuous military disasters Nicholas believed victory was near at hand. Losing his navy at Tsushima finally persuaded him to agree to peace negotiations. Even then he insisted on the option of reopening hostilities if peace conditions were unfavorable. He forbade his chief negotiator Count Witte to agree to either indemnity payments or loss of territory. Nicholas remained adamantly opposed to any concessions. Peace was made, but Witte did so by disobeying the tsar and ceding southern <a href="/wiki/Sakhalin" title="Sakhalin">Sakhalin</a> to Japan.<sup id="cite_ref-online_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-online-65">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (July 2019)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Anti-Jewish_pogroms_of_1903.E2.80.931906"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Anti-Jewish_pogroms_of_1903–1906">Anti-Jewish pogroms of 1903–1906</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Anti-Jewish_pogroms_in_the_Russian_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-Jewish pogroms in the Russian Empire">Anti-Jewish pogroms in the Russian Empire</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Kishinev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kishinev">Kishinev</a> newspaper <i>Bessarabets</i>, which published anti-Semitic materials, received funds from <a href="/wiki/Viacheslav_Plehve" class="mw-redirect" title="Viacheslav Plehve">Viacheslav Plehve</a>, Minister of the Interior.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> These publications served to fuel the <a href="/wiki/Kishinev_pogrom" title="Kishinev pogrom">Kishinev pogrom</a> (rioting). The government of Nicholas II formally condemned the rioting and dismissed the regional governor, with the perpetrators arrested and punished by the court.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Leadership of the Russian Orthodox Church also condemned anti-Semitic pogroms. Appeals to the faithful condemning the pogroms were read publicly in all churches of Russia.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> In private Nicholas expressed his admiration for the mobs, viewing anti-Semitism as a useful tool for unifying the people behind the government;<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> however in 1911, following the assassination of <a href="/wiki/Pyotr_Stolypin" title="Pyotr Stolypin">Pyotr Stolypin</a> by the Jewish revolutionary <a href="/wiki/Dmitry_Bogrov" title="Dmitry Bogrov">Dmitry Bogrov</a>, he approved of government efforts to prevent anti-Semitic pogroms.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Bloody_Sunday_.281905.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Bloody_Sunday_(1905)">Bloody Sunday (1905)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Bloody_Sunday_(1905)" title="Bloody Sunday (1905)">Bloody Sunday (1905)</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><video id="mwe_player_0" poster="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv/220px--Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv.jpg" controls="" preload="none" class="thumbimage" width="220" height="165" data-durationhint="18" data-mwtitle="Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_(1868-1917)_(1905).ogv" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/77/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv" type="video/ogg; codecs=&quot;theora, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Original Ogg file, 400 × 300 (544 kbps)" data-shorttitle="Ogg source" data-width="400" data-height="300" data-bandwidth="544308" data-framerate="29.97" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/77/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv.120p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Lowest bandwidth VP9 (120P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 120P" data-transcodekey="120p.vp9.webm" data-width="160" data-height="120" data-bandwidth="121768" data-framerate="29.97" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/77/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv.160p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth WebM (160P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 160P" data-transcodekey="160p.webm" data-width="214" data-height="160" data-bandwidth="122888" data-framerate="29.97" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/77/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv.180p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth VP9 (180P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 180P" data-transcodekey="180p.vp9.webm" data-width="240" data-height="180" data-bandwidth="200576" data-framerate="29.97" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/77/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv.240p.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="Small WebM (240P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.webm" data-width="320" data-height="240" data-bandwidth="238904" data-framerate="29.97" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/77/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv/Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_%281868-1917%29_%281905%29.ogv.240p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Small VP9 (240P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 240P" data-transcodekey="240p.vp9.webm" data-width="320" data-height="240" data-bandwidth="305128" data-framerate="29.97" /></video>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Czar_Nicholas_II_of_Russia_(1868-1917)_(1905).ogv" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Tsar Nicholas of Russia mounts his horse (1905?), unknown cinematographer of the <a href="/wiki/Edison_Manufacturing_Company" title="Edison Manufacturing Company">Edison Manufacturing Company</a></div></div></div>
<p>A few days prior to Bloody Sunday (9 (22) January 1905), priest and labor leader <a href="/wiki/Georgy_Gapon" title="Georgy Gapon">Georgy Gapon</a> informed the government of the forthcoming procession to the Winter Palace to hand a workers' petition to the Tsar. On Saturday, 8 (21) January, the ministers convened to consider the situation. There was never any thought that the Tsar, who had left the capital for Tsarskoye Selo on the advice of the ministers, would actually meet Gapon; the suggestion that some other member of the imperial family receive the petition was rejected.<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_R_p._124_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_R_p._124-75">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Finally informed by the Prefect of Police that he lacked the men to pluck Gapon from among his followers and place him under arrest, the newly appointed Minister of the Interior, Prince <a href="/wiki/Pyotr_Dmitrievich_Sviatopolk-Mirsky" class="mw-redirect" title="Pyotr Dmitrievich Sviatopolk-Mirsky">Sviatopolk-Mirsky</a>, and his colleagues decided to bring additional troops to reinforce the city. That evening Nicholas wrote in his diary, "Troops have been brought from the outskirts to reinforce the garrison. Up to now the workers have been calm. Their number is estimated at 120,000. At the head of their union is a kind of socialist priest named Gapon. Mirsky came this evening to present his report on the measures taken."<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_R_p._124_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_R_p._124-75">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On Sunday, 9 (22) January 1905, Gapon began his march. Locking arms, the workers <a href="/wiki/Nonviolent_resistance" title="Nonviolent resistance">marched peacefully</a> through the streets. Some carried religious icons and banners, as well as national flags and portraits of the Tsar. As they walked, they sang hymns and <i><a href="/wiki/God_Save_the_Tsar!" title="God Save the Tsar!">God Save The Tsar</a></i>. At 2<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>pm all of the converging processions were scheduled to arrive at the Winter Palace. There was no single confrontation with the troops. Throughout the city, at bridges on strategic boulevards, the marchers found their way blocked by lines of infantry, backed by <a href="/wiki/Cossack" class="mw-redirect" title="Cossack">Cossacks</a> and Hussars; and the soldiers opened fire on the crowd.<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_R_pp._124_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_R_pp._124-76">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The official number of victims was 92 dead and several hundred wounded. Gapon vanished and the other leaders of the march were seized. Expelled from the capital, they circulated through the empire, increasing the casualties. As bullets riddled their icons, their banners and their portraits of Nicholas, the people shrieked, "The Tsar will not help us!"<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_R_pp._124_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_R_pp._124-76">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Outside Russia, the future British Labour Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Ramsay_MacDonald" title="Ramsay MacDonald">Ramsay MacDonald</a> attacked the Tsar, calling him a "blood-stained creature and a common murderer".<sup id="cite_ref-massie125_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie125-77">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>That evening Nicholas wrote in his diary:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Difficult day! In St. Petersburg there were serious disturbances due to the desire of workers to get to the Winter Palace. The troops had to shoot in different places of the city, there were many dead and wounded. Lord, how painful and bad!<sup id="cite_ref-massie125_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie125-77">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-militera.lib.ru_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-militera.lib.ru-78">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>His younger sister, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Olga_Alexandrovna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Olga Alexandrovna of Russia">Grand Duchess Olga Alexandrovna</a>, wrote afterwards:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Nicky had the police report a few days before. That Saturday he telephoned my mother at the Anitchkov and said that she and I were to leave for Gatchina at once. He and Alicky went to Tsarskoye Selo. Insofar as I remember, my Uncles Vladimir and Nicholas were the only members of the family left in St. Petersburg, but there may have been others. I felt at the time that all those arrangements were hideously wrong. Nicky's ministers and the Chief of Police had it all their way. My mother and I wanted him to stay in St. Petersburg and to face the crowd. I am positive that, for all the ugly mood of some of the workmen, Nicky's appearance would have calmed them. They would have presented their petition and gone back to their homes. But that wretched Epiphany incident had left all the senior officials in a state of panic. They kept on telling Nicky that he had no right to run such a risk, that he owed it to the country to leave the capital, that even with the utmost precautions taken there might always be some loophole left. My mother and I did all we could to persuade him that the ministers' advice was wrong, but Nicky preferred to follow it and he was the first to repent when he heard of the tragic outcome.<sup id="cite_ref-vorres121_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vorres121-79">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>From his hiding place Gapon issued a letter, stating "Nicholas Romanov, formerly Tsar and at present soul-murderer of the Russian empire. The innocent blood of workers, their wives and children lies forever between you and the Russian people ... May all the blood which must be spilled fall upon you, you Hangman. I call upon all the socialist parties of Russia to come to an immediate agreement among themselves and bring an armed uprising against <a href="/wiki/Tsarism" class="mw-redirect" title="Tsarism">Tsarism</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-massie125_77-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie125-77">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1905_Revolution">1905 Revolution</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1905_Russian_Revolution" title="1905 Russian Revolution">1905 Russian Revolution</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg/250px-The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="113" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg/375px-The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg/500px-The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="864" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Church_Parade_of_the_Finnish_Life_Guards_Regiment.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II visiting the <a href="/wiki/Finland_Guard_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="Finland Guard Regiment">Finland Guard Regiment</a>, 1905</div></div></div>
<p>Confronted with growing opposition and after consulting with Witte and Prince Sviatopolk-Mirsky, the Tsar issued a reform <a href="/wiki/Ukase" title="Ukase">ukase</a> on 25 December 1904 with vague promises.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> 
In hopes of cutting the rebellion short, many demonstrators were shot on <a href="/wiki/Bloody_Sunday_(1905)" title="Bloody Sunday (1905)">Bloody Sunday (1905)</a> as they tried to march to the Winter Palace in St. Petersburg. <a href="/wiki/Dmitri_Feodorovich_Trepov" title="Dmitri Feodorovich Trepov">Dmitri Feodorovich Trepov</a> was ordered to take drastic measures to stop the revolutionary activity. <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Sergei_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Sergei Alexandrovich of Russia">Grand Duke Sergei</a> was killed in February by a revolutionary's bomb in Moscow as he left the Kremlin. On 3 March the Tsar condemned the revolutionaries. Meanwhile, Witte recommended that a manifesto be issued.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Schemes of reform would be elaborated by <a href="/wiki/Goremykin" class="mw-redirect" title="Goremykin">Goremykin</a> and a committee consisting of elected representatives of the <a href="/wiki/Zemstvos" class="mw-redirect" title="Zemstvos">zemstvos</a> and municipal councils under the presidency of Witte. In June the <a href="/wiki/Russian_battleship_Potemkin" title="Russian battleship Potemkin">battleship <i>Potemkin</i></a>, part of the <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet" title="Black Sea Fleet">Black Sea Fleet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mutiny" title="Mutiny">mutinied</a>.
