</p><p>Around August/September, after his diplomatic success on ending the <a href="/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War" title="Russo-Japanese War">Russo-Japanese War</a>, Witte wrote to the Tsar stressing the urgent need for political reforms at home. The Tsar remained quite impassive and indulgent; he spent most of that autumn hunting.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> With the defeat of Russia by a non-Western power, the prestige and authority of the autocratic regime fell significantly.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Tsar Nicholas II, taken by surprise by the events, reacted with anger and bewilderment. He wrote to his mother after months of disorder:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>It makes me sick to read the news! Nothing but strikes in schools and factories, murdered policemen, <a href="/wiki/Cossacks" title="Cossacks">Cossacks</a> and soldiers, riots, disorder, mutinies. But the ministers, instead of acting with quick decision, only assemble in council like a lot of frightened hens and cackle about providing united ministerial action... ominous quiet days began, quiet indeed because there was complete order in the streets, but at the same time everybody knew that something was going to happen&#160;— the troops were waiting for the signal, but the other side would not begin. One had the same feeling, as before a thunderstorm in summer! Everybody was on edge and extremely nervous and of course, that sort of strain could not go on for long.... We are in the midst of a revolution with an administrative apparatus entirely disorganized, and in this lies the main danger.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In October a railway strike developed into a <a href="/wiki/General_strike" title="General strike">general strike</a> which paralysed the country. In a city without electricity, Witte told Nicholas II "that the country was at the verge of a cataclysmic revolution".<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The Tsar accepted the draft, hurriedly outlined by Aleksei D. <a href="/wiki/Obolensky" title="Obolensky">Obolensky</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Emperor and Autocrat of All the Russias</i> was forced  to sign the <a href="/wiki/October_Manifesto" title="October Manifesto">October Manifesto</a> agreeing to the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/State_Duma_(Russian_Empire)" title="State Duma (Russian Empire)">Imperial Duma</a>, and to give up part of his unlimited autocracy. The freedom of religion clause outraged the Church because it allowed people to switch to evangelical Protestantism, which they denounced as heresy.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For the next six months, Witte was the <a href="/wiki/List_of_heads_of_government_of_Russia" title="List of heads of government of Russia">Prime Minister</a>. According to <a href="/wiki/Harold_Williams_(linguist)" title="Harold Williams (linguist)">Harold Williams</a>: "That government was almost paralyzed from the beginning." On 26 October (O.S.) the Tsar appointed Trepov Master of the Palace (without consulting Witte), and had daily contact with the Emperor; his influence at court was paramount. On 1 November 1905 (O.S.), <a href="/wiki/Princess_Milica_of_Montenegro" title="Princess Milica of Montenegro">Princess Milica of Montenegro</a> presented <a href="/wiki/Grigori_Rasputin" title="Grigori Rasputin">Grigori Rasputin</a> to Tsar Nicholas and his wife (who by then had a hemophiliac son) at <a href="/wiki/Peterhof_Palace" title="Peterhof Palace">Peterhof Palace</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relationship_with_the_Duma">Relationship with the Duma</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png/220px-1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="112" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png/330px-1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png/440px-1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png 2x" data-file-width="3010" data-file-height="1532" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:1_ruble_Nikolai_II_-_1913.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Silver_coin" title="Silver coin">Silver coin</a>: <b>1 ruble <a href="/wiki/Nikolai_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Nikolai II">Nikolai II</a>_<a href="/wiki/Romanov_Dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Romanov Dynasty">Romanov Dynasty</a> – 1913</b> – On the obverse of the coin features two rulers: left Emperor Nikolas II in military uniform of the life guards of the 4th infantry regiment of the Imperial family, right <a href="/wiki/Michael_of_Russia" title="Michael of Russia">Michael I</a> in Royal robes and Monomakh's Cap. Portraits made in a circular frame around of a Greek ornament.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Opening_of_Duma.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Opening_of_Duma.jpg/220px-Opening_of_Duma.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Opening_of_Duma.jpg/330px-Opening_of_Duma.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Opening_of_Duma.jpg/440px-Opening_of_Duma.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="892" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Opening_of_Duma.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II's opening speech before the two chambers of the State Duma in the <a href="/wiki/Winter_Palace" title="Winter Palace">Winter Palace</a>, 1906.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/0a/Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg/220px-Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="111" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/0a/Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg/330px-Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/0a/Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg/440px-Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg 2x" data-file-width="935" data-file-height="470" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_Coin.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>One ruble silver coin of Nicholas II, dated 1898, with the Imperial coat-of-arms on the reverse. The Russian inscription reads:<br /><i>B[ozheyu] M[ilostyu] Nikolay Imperator i Samoderzhets Vse[ya] Ross[ii].[iyskiy].</i><br />The English translation is, "By the grace of God, Nicholas II, Emperor and Autocrat of All the Russias."</div></div></div>
<p>Under pressure from the attempted <a href="/wiki/1905_Russian_Revolution" title="1905 Russian Revolution">1905 Russian Revolution</a>, on 5 August of that year Nicholas II issued a manifesto about the convocation of the <a href="/wiki/State_Duma_of_the_Russian_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="State Duma of the Russian Empire">State Duma</a>, known as the <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Bulygin" title="Alexander Bulygin">Bulygin Duma</a>, initially thought to be an advisory organ. In the October Manifesto, the Tsar pledged to introduce basic civil liberties, provide for broad participation in the State Duma, and endow the Duma with legislative and oversight powers. He was determined, however, to preserve his autocracy even in the context of reform. This was signalled in the text of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Constitution_of_1906" title="Russian Constitution of 1906">1906 constitution</a>. He was described as the supreme autocrat, and retained sweeping executive powers, also in church affairs. His cabinet ministers were not allowed to interfere with nor assist one another; they were responsible only to him.
</p><p>Nicholas's relations with the Duma were poor. The <a href="/wiki/First_Duma" class="mw-redirect" title="First Duma">First Duma</a>, with a majority of <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Democratic_party" class="mw-redirect" title="Constitutional Democratic party">Kadets</a>, almost immediately came into conflict with him. Scarcely had the 524 members sat down at the <a href="/wiki/Tauride_Palace" title="Tauride Palace">Tauride Palace</a> when they formulated an 'Address to the Throne'. It demanded <a href="/wiki/Universal_suffrage" title="Universal suffrage">universal suffrage</a>, radical land reform, the release of all <a href="/wiki/Political_prisoner" title="Political prisoner">political prisoners</a> and the dismissal of ministers appointed by the Tsar in favour of ministers acceptable to the Duma.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Grand Duchess Olga, Nicholas's sister, later wrote:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>There was such gloom at Tsarskoye Selo. I did not understand anything about politics. I just felt everything was going wrong with the country and all of us. The October Constitution did not seem to satisfy anyone. I went with my mother to the first Duma. I remember the large group of deputies from among peasants and factory people. The peasants looked sullen. But the workmen were worse: they looked as though they hated us. I remember the distress in <a href="/wiki/Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)" title="Alexandra Feodorovna (Alix of Hesse)">Alicky</a>'s eyes.<sup id="cite_ref-vorres121_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vorres121-79">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Minister of the Court Count <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Frederiks" class="mw-redirect" title="Vladimir Frederiks">Vladimir Frederiks</a> commented, "The Deputies, they give one the impression of a gang of criminals who are only waiting for the signal to throw themselves upon the ministers and cut their throats. I will never again set foot among those people."<sup id="cite_ref-massie242_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie242-91">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> The Dowager Empress noticed "incomprehensible hatred."<sup id="cite_ref-massie242_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie242-91">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Nicholas initially had a good relationship with his prime minister, Sergei Witte, Alexandra distrusted him as he had instigated an investigation of Grigori Rasputin and, as the political situation deteriorated, Nicholas dissolved the Duma. The Duma was populated with <a href="/wiki/Political_radicalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Political radicalism">radicals</a>, many of whom wished to push through legislation that would abolish private property ownership, among other things. Witte, unable to grasp the seemingly insurmountable problems of reforming Russia and the monarchy, wrote to Nicholas on 14 April 1906 resigning his office (however, other accounts have said that Witte was forced to resign by the Emperor). Nicholas was not ungracious to Witte and an Imperial Rescript was published on 22 April creating Witte a Knight of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Alexander_Nevsky" title="Order of Saint Alexander Nevsky">Order of Saint Alexander Nevsky</a> with diamonds (the last two words were written in the Emperor's own hand, followed by "I remain unalterably well-disposed to you and sincerely grateful, for ever more Nicholas.").
</p><p>A second Duma met for the first time in February 1907. The leftist parties—including the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Social_Democratic_Labour_Party" title="Russian Social Democratic Labour Party">Social Democrats</a> and the Social Revolutionaries, who had boycotted the First Duma—had won 200 seats in the Second, more than a third of the membership. Again Nicholas waited impatiently to rid himself of the Duma. In two letters to his mother he let his bitterness flow:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>A grotesque deputation is coming from England to see liberal members of the Duma. <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward VII of the United Kingdom">Uncle Bertie</a> informed us that they were very sorry but were unable to take action to stop their coming. Their famous "liberty", of course. How angry they would be if a deputation went from us to the Irish to wish them success in their struggle against their government.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>A little while later he further wrote:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>All would be well if everything said in the Duma remained within its walls. Every word spoken, however, comes out in the next day's papers which are avidly read by everyone. In many places the populace is getting restive again. They begin to talk about land once more and are waiting to see what the Duma is going to say on the question. I am getting telegrams from everywhere, petitioning me to order a dissolution, but it is too early for that. One has to let them do something manifestly stupid or mean and then&#160;— slap! And they are gone!<sup id="cite_ref-massie245_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie245-93">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Stolypin,_Nicholas_II,_jewish_delegation.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Stolypin%2C_Nicholas_II%2C_jewish_delegation.jpg/250px-Stolypin%2C_Nicholas_II%2C_jewish_delegation.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="185" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Stolypin%2C_Nicholas_II%2C_jewish_delegation.jpg/375px-Stolypin%2C_Nicholas_II%2C_jewish_delegation.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Stolypin%2C_Nicholas_II%2C_jewish_delegation.jpg/500px-Stolypin%2C_Nicholas_II%2C_jewish_delegation.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1530" data-file-height="1135" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Stolypin,_Nicholas_II,_jewish_delegation.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II, Stolypin and the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Ukraine" title="History of the Jews in Ukraine">Jewish</a> delegation during the Tsar's visit to <a href="/wiki/Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiev">Kiev</a> in 1911</div></div></div>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/Second_Duma" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Duma">Second Duma</a> resulted in similar problems, the new prime minister <a href="/wiki/Pyotr_Stolypin" title="Pyotr Stolypin">Pyotr Stolypin</a> (whom Witte described as "reactionary") unilaterally dissolved it, and changed the electoral laws to allow for future Dumas to have a more conservative content, and to be dominated by the liberal-conservative <a href="/wiki/Octobrist" class="mw-redirect" title="Octobrist">Octobrist</a> Party of <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Guchkov" title="Alexander Guchkov">Alexander Guchkov</a>. Stolypin, a skilful politician, had ambitious plans for reform. These included making loans available to the lower classes to enable them to buy land, with the intent of forming a farming class loyal to the crown. Nevertheless, when the Duma remained hostile, Stolypin had no qualms about invoking Article 87 of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Constitution_of_1906" title="Russian Constitution of 1906">Fundamental Laws</a>, which empowered the Tsar to issue 'urgent and extraordinary' emergency decrees 'during the recess of the State Duma'. Stolypin's most famous legislative act, the change in peasant land tenure, was promulgated under Article 87.<sup id="cite_ref-massie245_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie245-93">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The third Duma remained an independent body. This time the members proceeded cautiously. Instead of hurling themselves at the government, opposing parties within the Duma worked to develop the body as a whole. In the classic manner of the British Parliament, the Duma reached for power grasping for the national purse strings. The Duma had the right to question ministers behind closed doors as to their proposed expenditures. These sessions, endorsed by Stolypin, were educational for both sides, and, in time, mutual antagonism was replaced by mutual respect. Even the sensitive area of military expenditure, where the October Manifesto clearly had reserved decisions to the throne, a Duma commission began to operate. Composed of aggressive patriots no less anxious than Nicholas to restore the fallen honour of Russian arms, the Duma commission frequently recommended expenditures even larger than those proposed.
</p><p>With the passage of time, Nicholas also began to have confidence in the Duma. "This Duma cannot be reproached with an attempt to seize power and there is no need at all to quarrel with it," he said to Stolypin in 1909.<sup id="cite_ref-massie246_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie246-94">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Stolypin's plans were undercut by conservatives at court. Although the tsar at first supported him, he finally sided with the arch critics.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Reactionaries such as Prince <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Nikolayevich_Orlov" title="Vladimir Nikolayevich Orlov">Vladimir Nikolayevich Orlov</a> never tired of telling the tsar that the very existence of the Duma was a blot on the autocracy. Stolypin, they whispered, was a traitor and secret revolutionary who was conniving with the Duma to steal the prerogatives assigned the Tsar by God. Witte also engaged in constant intrigue against Stolypin. Although Stolypin had had nothing to do with Witte's fall, Witte blamed him. Stolypin had unwittingly angered the Tsaritsa. He had ordered an investigation into Rasputin and presented it to the Tsar, who read it but did nothing. Stolypin, on his own authority, ordered Rasputin to leave St. Petersburg. Alexandra protested vehemently but Nicholas refused to overrule his Prime Minister,<sup id="cite_ref-massie247_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie247-96">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> who had more influence with the Emperor.
</p><p>By the time of Stolypin's assassination in September 1911, Stolypin had grown weary of the burdens of office. For a man who preferred clear decisive action, working with a sovereign who believed in fatalism and mysticism was frustrating. As an example, Nicholas once returned a document unsigned with the note:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Despite most convincing arguments in favour of adopting a positive decision in this matter, an inner voice keeps on insisting more and more that I do not accept responsibility for it. So far my conscience has not deceived me. Therefore I intend in this case to follow its dictates. I know that you, too, believe that "a Tsar's heart is in God's hands." Let it be so. For all laws established by me I bear a great responsibility before God, and I am ready to answer for my decision at any time.<sup id="cite_ref-massie247_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie247-96">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Rasputin_PA.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Rasputin_PA.jpg/220px-Rasputin_PA.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="290" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Rasputin_PA.jpg/330px-Rasputin_PA.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Rasputin_PA.jpg/440px-Rasputin_PA.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2276" data-file-height="3000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Rasputin_PA.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Grigori_Rasputin" title="Grigori Rasputin">Grigori Rasputin</a></div></div></div>
<p>Alexandra, believing that Stolypin had severed the bonds that her son depended on for life, hated the Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-massie247_96-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie247-96">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In March 1911, in a fit of anger stating that he no longer commanded the imperial confidence, Stolypin asked to be relieved of his office. Two years earlier when Stolypin had casually mentioned resigning to Nicholas he was informed: "This is not a question of confidence or lack of it. It is my will. Remember that we live in Russia, not abroad...and therefore I shall not consider the possibility of any resignation."<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> He was assassinated in September 1911.
</p><p>In 1912, a fourth Duma was elected with almost the same membership as the third. "The Duma started too fast. Now it is slower, but better, and more lasting," stated Nicholas to Sir <a href="/wiki/Bernard_Pares" title="Bernard Pares">Bernard Pares</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-massie246_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-massie246-94">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a> developed badly for Russia. By late 1916, Romanov family desperation reached the point that <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Paul_Alexandrovich" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Duke Paul Alexandrovich">Grand Duke Paul Alexandrovich</a>, younger brother of Alexander III and the Tsar's only surviving uncle, was deputed to beg Nicholas to grant a constitution and a government responsible to the Duma. Nicholas sternly and adamantly refused, reproaching his uncle for asking him to break his coronation oath to maintain autocratic power for his successors. In the Duma on 2 December 1916, Vladimir Purishkevich, a fervent patriot, monarchist and war worker, denounced the dark forces which surrounded the throne in a thunderous two-hour speech which was tumultuously applauded. "Revolution threatens," he warned, "and an obscure peasant shall govern Russia no longer!"<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Tsarevich_Alexei.27s_illness_and_Rasputin"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Tsarevich_Alexei's_illness_and_Rasputin">Tsarevich Alexei's illness and Rasputin</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexei_Nikolaevich,_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Alexei_Nikolaevich%2C_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg/220px-Alexei_Nikolaevich%2C_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="349" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Alexei_Nikolaevich%2C_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg/330px-Alexei_Nikolaevich%2C_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Alexei_Nikolaevich%2C_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg/440px-Alexei_Nikolaevich%2C_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1209" data-file-height="1920" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexei_Nikolaevich,_Tsarevich_of_Russia.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Alexei in 1913</div></div></div>
<p>Further complicating domestic matters was the matter of the succession. Alexandra bore Nicholas four daughters, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Olga_Nikolaevna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Olga Nikolaevna of Russia">the Grand Duchess Olga</a> in 1895, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Tatiana_Nikolaevna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Tatiana Nikolaevna of Russia">the Grand Duchess Tatiana</a> in 1897, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Maria_Nikolaevna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Maria Nikolaevna of Russia">Grand Duchess Maria</a> in 1899, and <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Anastasia_Nikolaevna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Anastasia Nikolaevna of Russia">Grand Duchess Anastasia</a> in 1901, before their son <a href="/wiki/Alexei_Nikolaevich,_Tsarevich_of_Russia" title="Alexei Nikolaevich, Tsarevich of Russia">Alexei</a> was born on 12 August 1904. The young heir was afflicted with <a href="/wiki/Hemophilia_B" class="mw-redirect" title="Hemophilia B">Hemophilia B</a>, a hereditary disease that prevents blood from clotting properly, which at that time was untreatable and usually led to an untimely death. As a granddaughter of Queen Victoria, Alexandra carried the same <a href="/wiki/Gene_mutation" class="mw-redirect" title="Gene mutation">gene mutation</a> that afflicted several of the major European royal houses, such as <a href="/wiki/Prussia" title="Prussia">Prussia</a> and Spain. Hemophilia, therefore, became known as "<a href="/wiki/Hemophilia_in_European_royalty" class="mw-redirect" title="Hemophilia in European royalty">the royal disease</a>". Through Alexandra, the disease had passed on to her son. As all of Nicholas and Alexandra's daughters were assassinated with their parents and brother in Yekaterinburg in 1918, it is not known whether any of them inherited the gene as <a href="/wiki/Genetic_carrier" class="mw-redirect" title="Genetic carrier">carriers</a>.
</p><p>Before Rasputin's arrival, the tsarina and the tsar had consulted numerous mystics, charlatans, "holy fools", and miracle workers. The royal behavior was not some odd aberration, but a deliberate retreat from the secular social and economic forces of his time – an act of faith and vote of confidence in a spiritual past. They had set themselves up for the greatest spiritual advisor and manipulator in Russian history.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Because of the fragility of the autocracy at this time, Nicholas and Alexandra chose to keep secret Alexei's condition. Even within the household, many were unaware of the exact nature of the Tsarevich's illness. At first Alexandra turned to Russian doctors and medics to treat Alexei; however, their treatments generally failed, and Alexandra increasingly turned to <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystics</a> and holy men (or <i><a href="/wiki/Starets" title="Starets">starets</a></i> as they were called in Russian). One of these starets, an illiterate Siberian named Grigori Rasputin, gained amazing success. Rasputin's influence over Empress Alexandra, and consequently the Tsar himself, grew even stronger after 1912 when the Tsarevich nearly died from an injury. His bleeding grew steadily worse as doctors despaired, and priests administered the <a href="/wiki/Last_rites" title="Last rites">Last Sacrament</a>. In desperation, Alexandra called upon Rasputin, to which he replied, "God has seen your tears and heard your prayers. Do not grieve. The Little One will not die. Do not allow the doctors to bother him too much."<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The hemorrhage stopped the very next day and the boy began to recover. Alexandra took this as a sign that Rasputin was a <i>starets</i> and that God was with him; for the rest of her life she would fervently defend him and turn her wrath against anyone who dared to question him.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="European_affairs">European affairs</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg/250px-Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="179" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg/375px-Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg/500px-Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1434" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_and_Alexei_in_Reval_1908.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II and his son Alexei aboard the Imperial yacht <i><a href="/wiki/Russian_yacht_Standart" title="Russian yacht Standart">Standart</a></i>, during King <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">Edward VII</a>'s state visit to Russia in <a href="/wiki/Tallinn" title="Tallinn">Reval</a>, 1908</div></div></div>
<p>In 1907, to end longstanding controversies over central Asia, Russia and the United Kingdom signed the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Russian_Convention_of_1907" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-Russian Convention of 1907">Anglo-Russian Convention</a> that resolved most of the problems generated for decades by <a href="/wiki/The_Great_Game" class="mw-redirect" title="The Great Game">The Great Game</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The UK had already entered into the <a href="/wiki/Entente_cordiale" class="mw-redirect" title="Entente cordiale">Entente cordiale</a> with France in 1904, and the Anglo-Russian convention led to the formation of the <a href="/wiki/Triple_Entente" title="Triple Entente">Triple Entente</a>. The following year, in May 1908, Nicholas and Alexandra's shared "Uncle Bertie" and "Aunt Alix", Britain's King Edward VII and Queen Alexandra, made a state visit to Russia, being the first reigning British monarchs to do so. However, they did not set foot on Russian soil. Instead, they stayed aboard their yachts, meeting off the coast of modern-day <a href="/wiki/Tallinn" title="Tallinn">Tallinn</a>. Later that year, Nicholas was taken off guard by the news that his foreign minister, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Izvolsky" title="Alexander Izvolsky">Alexander Izvolsky</a>, had entered into a secret agreement with the Austro-Hungarian foreign minister, <a href="/wiki/Count_Alois_Lexa_von_Aehrenthal" class="mw-redirect" title="Count Alois Lexa von Aehrenthal">Count Alois von Aehrenthal</a>, agreeing that, in exchange for Russian naval access to the <a href="/wiki/Dardanelles" title="Dardanelles">Dardanelles</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Bosporus_Strait" class="mw-redirect" title="Bosporus Strait">Bosporus Strait</a>, Russia would not oppose the Austrian annexation of <a href="/wiki/Bosnia_and_Herzegovina" title="Bosnia and Herzegovina">Bosnia and Herzegovina</a>, a revision of the 1878 <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Berlin_(1878)" title="Treaty of Berlin (1878)">Treaty of Berlin</a>. When Austria-Hungary did annex this territory that October, it precipitated the <a href="/wiki/Bosnian_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Bosnian crisis">diplomatic crisis</a>. When Russia protested about the annexation, the Austrians threatened to leak secret communications between Izvolsky and Aehernthal, prompting Nicholas to complain in a letter to the Austrian emperor, <a href="/wiki/Franz_Joseph_I_of_Austria" title="Franz Joseph I of Austria">Franz Joseph</a>, about a breach of confidence. In 1909, in the wake of the Anglo-Russian convention, the Russian imperial family made a visit to England, staying on the <a href="/wiki/Isle_of_Wight" title="Isle of Wight">Isle of Wight</a> for <a href="/wiki/Cowes_Week" title="Cowes Week">Cowes Week</a>. In 1913, during the <a href="/wiki/Balkan_War_(1912%E2%80%9313)" class="mw-redirect" title="Balkan War (1912–13)">Balkan Wars</a>, Nicholas personally offered to arbitrate between Serbia and Bulgaria. However, the Bulgarians rejected his offer. Also in 1913, Nicholas, albeit without Alexandra, made a visit to Berlin for the wedding of Kaiser Wilhelm II's daughter, <a href="/wiki/Princess_Victoria_Louise_of_Prussia" title="Princess Victoria Louise of Prussia">Princess Victoria Louise</a>, to a maternal cousin of Nicholas, <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Augustus,_Duke_of_Brunswick" title="Ernest Augustus, Duke of Brunswick">Ernest Augustus, the Duke of Brunswick</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas was also joined by his cousin, <a href="/wiki/King_George_V" class="mw-redirect" title="King George V">King George V</a> and his wife, <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Teck" title="Mary of Teck">Queen Mary</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tercentenary">Tercentenary</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Romanov_Tercentenary" title="Romanov Tercentenary">Romanov Tercentenary</a></div>
<p>In February 1913, Nicholas presided over the <a href="/wiki/Romanov_Tercentenary" title="Romanov Tercentenary">tercentenary celebrations for the Romanov Dynasty</a>. On 21 February, a <i><a href="/wiki/Te_Deum" title="Te Deum">Te Deum</a></i> took place at Kazan Cathedral, and a state reception at the Winter Palace.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In May, Nicholas and the imperial family made a pilgrimage across the empire, retracing the route down the <a href="/wiki/Volga_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Volga River">Volga River</a> that was made by the teenage <a href="/wiki/Michael_I_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Michael I of Russia">Michael Romanov</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Ipatiev_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Ipatiev Monastery">Ipatiev Monastery</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kostroma" title="Kostroma">Kostroma</a> to Moscow in 1613 when he finally agreed to become Tsar.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Finland" title="Grand Duchy of Finland">Finland</a>, Nicholas had become associated with deeply unpopular <a href="/wiki/Russification_of_Finland" title="Russification of Finland">Russification measures</a>. These began with the <a href="/wiki/February_Manifesto" title="February Manifesto">February Manifesto</a> proclaimed by Nicholas II in 1899,<sup id="cite_ref-wargelin_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wargelin-105">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> which restricted Finland's <a href="/wiki/Autonomy" title="Autonomy">autonomy</a> and instigated a period of censorship and political repression.<sup id="cite_ref-clements_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-clements-106">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> A petition of protest signed by more than 500,000 Finns was collected against the manifesto and delivered to St. Petersburg by a delegation of 500 people, but they were not received by Nicholas. Russification measures were reintroduced in 1908 after a temporary suspension in the aftermath of the 1905 Revolution, and Nicholas received an icy reception when he made his only visit to <a href="/wiki/Helsinki" title="Helsinki">Helsinki</a> on 10 March 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_World_War">First World War</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Russian_entry_into_World_War_I" title="Russian entry into World War I">Russian entry into World War I</a></div>
<p>On 28 June 1914 <a href="/wiki/Archduke_Franz_Ferdinand_of_Austria" title="Archduke Franz Ferdinand of Austria">Archduke Franz Ferdinand of Austria</a>, heir to the <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austro-Hungarian</a> throne, was assassinated by a Bosnian Serb nationalist in <a href="/wiki/Sarajevo" title="Sarajevo">Sarajevo</a>, who opposed Austria-Hungary's <a href="/wiki/Bosnian_Crisis" title="Bosnian Crisis">annexation of Bosnia-Herzegovina</a>. The outbreak of war was not inevitable, but leaders, diplomats and nineteenth-century alliances created a climate for large-scale conflict. The concept of <a href="/wiki/Pan-Slavism" title="Pan-Slavism">Pan-Slavism</a> and shared religion created strong public sympathy between Russia and Serbia. Territorial conflict created rivalries between <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">Germany</a> and France and between Austria-Hungary and <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a>, and as a consequence alliance networks developed across Europe. The <a href="/wiki/Triple_Entente" title="Triple Entente">Triple Entente</a> and <a href="/wiki/Triple_Alliance_(1882)" title="Triple Alliance (1882)">Triple Alliance</a> networks were set before the war. Nicholas wanted neither to abandon Serbia to the ultimatum of Austria, nor to provoke a general war. In a series of letters exchanged with <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_II,_German_Emperor" title="Wilhelm II, German Emperor">Wilhelm</a> of Germany (the "<a href="/wiki/Willy%E2%80%93Nicky_correspondence" title="Willy–Nicky correspondence">Willy–Nicky correspondence</a>") the two proclaimed their desire for peace, and each attempted to get the other to back down. Nicholas desired that Russia's mobilization be only against Austria-Hungary, in the hopes of preventing war with Germany.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302,_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302%2C_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg/220px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302%2C_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="349" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302%2C_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg/330px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302%2C_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302%2C_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg/440px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302%2C_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg 2x" data-file-width="1099" data-file-height="1741" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-R43302,_Kaiser_Wilhelm_II._und_Zar_Nikolaus_II..jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II (right) with <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_II,_German_Emperor" title="Wilhelm II, German Emperor">Kaiser Wilhelm II</a> of Germany in 1905. Nicholas is wearing a German Army uniform, while Wilhelm wears that of a Russian hussar regiment.</div></div></div>
<p>On 25 July 1914, at his council of ministers, Nicholas decided to intervene in the Austro-Serbian conflict, a step toward general war. He put the Russian army on "alert"<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceC_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceC-110">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> on 25 July. Although this was not general mobilization, it threatened the German and Austro-Hungarian borders and looked like military preparation for war.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceC_110-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceC-110">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> However, his army had no contingency plans for a partial mobilization, and on 30 July 1914 Nicholas took the fateful step of confirming the order for general mobilization, despite being strongly counselled against it.
</p><p>On 28 July, Austria-Hungary formally declared war against Serbia. On 29 July 1914, Nicholas sent a telegram to Wilhelm with the suggestion to submit the Austro-Serbian problem to the <a href="/wiki/Hague_Conventions_of_1899_and_1907" title="Hague Conventions of 1899 and 1907">Hague Conference</a> (in <a href="/wiki/Permanent_Court_of_Arbitration" title="Permanent Court of Arbitration">Hague tribunal</a>). Wilhelm did not address the question of the Hague Conference in his subsequent reply.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Count Witte told the French Ambassador, <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Pal%C3%A9ologue" title="Maurice Paléologue">Maurice Paléologue</a> that from Russia's point of view the war was madness, Slav solidarity was simply nonsense and Russia could hope for nothing from the war.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> On 30 July, Russia ordered general mobilization, but still maintained that it would not attack if peace talks were to begin. Germany, reacting to the discovery of partial mobilization ordered on 25 July, announced its own pre-mobilization posture, the Imminent Danger of War. Germany requested that Russia demobilize within the next twelve hours.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> In Saint Petersburg, at 7&#160;pm, with the ultimatum to Russia having expired, the German ambassador to Russia met with the Russian Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Sergey_Sazonov" title="Sergey Sazonov">Sergey Sazonov</a>, asked three times if Russia would reconsider, and then with shaking hands, delivered the note accepting Russia's war challenge and declaring war on 1 August. Less than a week later, on 6 August, Franz Joseph signed the Austro-Hungarian declaration of war on Russia.
</p><p>The outbreak of war on 1 August 1914 found Russia grossly unprepared. Russia and her allies placed their faith in her army, the famous 'Russian steamroller'.<sup id="cite_ref-tames42_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames42-115">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Its pre-war regular strength was 1,400,000; mobilization added 3,100,000 reserves and millions more stood ready behind them. In every other respect, however, Russia was unprepared for war. Germany had ten times as much railway track per square mile, and whereas Russian soldiers travelled an average of 1,290 kilometres (800&#160;mi) to reach the front, German soldiers traveled less than a quarter of that distance. Russian heavy industry was still too small to equip the massive armies the Tsar could raise, and her reserves of munitions were pitifully small; while the German army in 1914 was better equipped than any other, man-for-man, the Russians were severely short on artillery pieces, shells, motorized transports, and even boots. With the Baltic Sea barred by German U-boats and the Dardanelles by the guns of Germany's ally, the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>, Russia initially could receive help only via <a href="/wiki/Arkhangelsk" title="Arkhangelsk">Archangel</a>, which was frozen solid in winter, or via <a href="/wiki/Vladivostok" title="Vladivostok">Vladivostok</a>, which was over 6,400 kilometres (4,000&#160;mi) from the front line. By 1915, a rail line was built north from <a href="/wiki/Petrozavodsk" title="Petrozavodsk">Petrozavodsk</a> to the Kola Gulf and this connection laid the foundation of the ice-free port of what eventually was called <a href="/wiki/Murmansk" title="Murmansk">Murmansk</a>. The Russian High Command was moreover greatly weakened by the mutual contempt between <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Sukhomlinov" title="Vladimir Sukhomlinov">Vladimir Sukhomlinov</a>, the Minister of War, and the incompetent Grand Duke Nicholas Nikolayevich who commanded the armies in the field.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> In spite of all of this, an immediate attack was ordered against the German province of <a href="/wiki/East_Prussia" title="East Prussia">East Prussia</a>. The Germans mobilised there with great efficiency and completely defeated the two Russian armies which had invaded. The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tannenberg_(1914)" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Tannenberg (1914)">Battle of Tannenberg</a>, where an entire Russian army was annihilated, cast an ominous shadow over Russia's future. Russia had great success against both the Austro-Hungarian and Ottoman armies from the very beginning of the war, but they never succeeded against the might of the German Army. In September 1914, to relieve pressure on France, the Russians were forced to halt a successful offensive against Austria-Hungary in <a href="/wiki/Galicia_(Eastern_Europe)" title="Galicia (Eastern Europe)">Galicia</a> to attack German-held Silesia.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:282px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg/280px-Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg/420px-Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg/560px-Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="474" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Russian_prisoners_tannenberg.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Russian prisoners after the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tannenberg" title="Battle of Tannenberg">Battle of Tannenberg</a>, where the <a href="/wiki/2nd_Army_(Russian_Empire)" title="2nd Army (Russian Empire)">Russian Second Army</a> was annihilated by the <a href="/wiki/8th_Army_(German_Empire)" title="8th Army (German Empire)">German Eighth Army</a></div></div></div>
<p>Gradually a war of attrition set in on the vast <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Front_(World_War_I)" title="Eastern Front (World War I)">Eastern Front</a>, where the Russians were facing the combined forces of the German and Austro-Hungarian armies, and they suffered staggering losses. General Denikin, retreating from Galicia wrote, "The German heavy artillery swept away whole lines of trenches, and their defenders with them. We hardly replied. There was nothing with which we could reply. Our regiments, although completely exhausted, were beating off one attack after another by bayonet ... Blood flowed unendingly, the ranks became thinner and thinner and thinner. The number of graves multiplied."<sup id="cite_ref-tames46_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames46-118">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> On 5 August, with the Russian army in retreat, <a href="/wiki/Warsaw" title="Warsaw">Warsaw</a> fell. Defeat at the front bred disorder at home. At first, the targets were German, and for three days in June shops, bakeries, factories, private houses and country estates belonging to people with German names were looted and burned.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2011)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The inflamed mobs then turned on the government, declaring the Empress should be shut up in a convent, the Tsar deposed and Rasputin hanged. Nicholas was by no means deaf to these discontents. An emergency session of the Duma was summoned and a Special Defense Council established, its members drawn from the Duma and the Tsar's ministers.
</p><p>In July 1915, King <a href="/wiki/Christian_X_of_Denmark" title="Christian X of Denmark">Christian X</a> of <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a>, first cousin of the Tsar, sent <a href="/wiki/Hans_Niels_Andersen" title="Hans Niels Andersen">Hans Niels Andersen</a> to Tsarskoye Selo with an offer to act as a mediator. He made several trips between London, Berlin and Petrograd and in July saw the Dowager Empress <a href="/wiki/Maria_Feodorovna_(Dagmar_of_Denmark)" title="Maria Feodorovna (Dagmar of Denmark)">Maria Fyodorovna</a>. Andersen told her they should conclude peace. Nicholas chose to turn down King Christian's offer of mediation, as he felt it would be a betrayal for Russia to form a separate peace treaty with the Central Powers when its allies Britain and France were still fighting.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The energetic and efficient General <a href="/wiki/Alexei_Polivanov" title="Alexei Polivanov">Alexei Polivanov</a> replaced Sukhomlinov as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of War">Minister of War</a>, which failed to improve the strategic situation.<sup id="cite_ref-tames42_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames42-115">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> In the aftermath of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Retreat_(Russia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Retreat (Russia)">Great Retreat</a> and the loss of the Kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>, Nicholas assumed the role of commander-in-chief after dismissing his cousin, Grand Duke Nicholas Nikolayevich, in September 1915. This was a mistake, as the Tsar came to be personally associated with the continuing losses at the front. He was also away at the remote HQ at <a href="/wiki/Mogilev" title="Mogilev">Mogilev</a>, far from the direct governance of the empire, and when revolution broke out in <a href="/wiki/Petrograd" class="mw-redirect" title="Petrograd">Petrograd</a> he was unable to halt it. In reality the move was largely symbolic, since all important military decisions were made by his chief-of-staff General <a href="/wiki/Michael_Alexeiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Michael Alexeiev">Michael Alexeiev</a>, and Nicholas did little more than review troops, inspect <a href="/wiki/Field_hospital" title="Field hospital">field hospitals</a>, and preside over military luncheons.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg/250px-Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="179" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg/375px-Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg/500px-Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5183" data-file-height="3707" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II with his family in <a href="/wiki/Yevpatoria" title="Yevpatoria">Yevpatoria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Crimea" title="Crimea">Crimea</a>, May 1916</div></div></div>
<p>The Duma was still calling for political reforms and political unrest continued throughout the war. Cut off from public opinion, Nicholas could not see that the dynasty was tottering. With Nicholas at the front, domestic issues and control of the capital were left with his wife Alexandra. However, Alexandra's relationship with Grigori Rasputin, and her German background, further discredited the dynasty's authority. Nicholas had been repeatedly warned about the destructive influence of Rasputin but had failed to remove him. Rumors and accusations about Alexandra and Rasputin appeared one after another; Alexandra was even accused of harboring treasonous sympathies towards Germany. Anger at Nicholas's failure to act and the extreme damage that Rasputin's influence was doing to Russia's war effort and to the monarchy led to Rasputin's eventual murder by a group of nobles, led by Prince <a href="/wiki/Felix_Yusupov" title="Felix Yusupov">Felix Yusupov</a> and <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Dmitri_Pavlovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Dmitri Pavlovich of Russia">Grand Duke Dmitri Pavlovich</a>, a cousin of the Tsar, in the early morning of Saturday 17 December 1916 (<a href="/wiki/Old_Style" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Style">O.S.</a>) / 30 December 1916 (<a href="/wiki/New_Style" class="mw-redirect" title="New Style">N.S.</a>).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Collapse">Collapse</span></h3>
