//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%B4%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%93%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%BC%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B4%D1%83%D1%8E%D1%89%D0%B5%D0%B3%D0%BE._%D0%9C%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%B2._1_%D0%B0%D0%BF%D1%80.1916.jpg/440px-%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%B4%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%93%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%BC%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B4%D1%83%D1%8E%D1%89%D0%B5%D0%B3%D0%BE._%D0%9C%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%B2._1_%D0%B0%D0%BF%D1%80.1916.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="711" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%B4%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%B5_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%BA%D0%B8_%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%93%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%BC%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B4%D1%83%D1%8E%D1%89%D0%B5%D0%B3%D0%BE._%D0%9C%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%B2._1_%D0%B0%D0%BF%D1%80.1916.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas with members of the <a href="/wiki/Stavka" title="Stavka">Stavka</a> at Mogilev in April 1916.</div></div></div>
<p>As the government failed to produce supplies, mounting hardship resulted in massive riots and rebellions. With Nicholas away at the front from 1915 through 1916, authority appeared to collapse and the capital was left in the hands of strikers and mutineering soldiers. Despite efforts by the British Ambassador <a href="/wiki/George_Buchanan_(diplomat)" title="George Buchanan (diplomat)">Sir George Buchanan</a> to warn the Tsar that he should grant constitutional reforms to fend off revolution, Nicholas continued to bury himself away at the Staff HQ (<a href="/wiki/Stavka" title="Stavka">Stavka</a>) 600 kilometres (400&#160;mi) away at Mogilev, leaving his capital and court open to intrigues and insurrection.<sup id="cite_ref-tames52_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames52-121">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ideologically the tsar's greatest support came from the right-wing monarchists, who had recently gained strength. However they were increasingly alienated by the tsar's support of Stolypin's Westernizing reforms taken early in the Revolution of 1905 and especially by the political power the tsar had bestowed on Rasputin.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By early 1917, Russia was on the verge of total collapse of morale. An estimated 1.7&#160;million Russian soldiers <a href="/wiki/World_War_I_casualties" title="World War I casualties">were killed in World War I</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> The sense of failure and imminent disaster was everywhere. The army had taken 15 million men from the farms and food prices had soared. An egg cost four times what it had in 1914, butter five times as much. The severe winter dealt the railways, overburdened by emergency shipments of coal and supplies, a crippling blow.<sup id="cite_ref-tames52_121-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames52-121">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Russia entered the war with 20,000 locomotives; by 1917, 9,000 were in service, while the number of serviceable railway wagons had dwindled from half a million to 170,000. In February 1917, 1,200 locomotives burst their boilers and nearly 60,000 wagons were immobilized. In Petrograd, supplies of flour and fuel had all but disappeared.<sup id="cite_ref-tames52_121-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames52-121">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> War-time <a href="/wiki/Prohibition_of_alcohol" class="mw-redirect" title="Prohibition of alcohol">prohibition of alcohol</a> was enacted by Nicholas to boost patriotism and productivity, but instead damaged the funding of the war, due to the treasury now being deprived of alcohol taxes.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-124">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 23 February 1917 in Petrograd, a combination of very severe cold weather and acute food shortages caused people to break into shops and bakeries to get bread and other necessities. In the streets, red banners appeared and the crowds chanted "Down with the German woman! Down with <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Protopopov" title="Alexander Protopopov">Protopopov</a>! Down with the war! Down with the Tsar!"<sup id="cite_ref-tames52_121-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames52-121">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Police shot at the populace which incited riots. The troops in the capital were poorly motivated and their officers had no reason to be loyal to the regime, with the bulk of the tsar's loyalists away fighting World War I. In contrast, the soldiers in Petrograd were angry, full of revolutionary fervor and sided with the populace.<sup id="cite_ref-tames53_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames53-125">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Tsar's Cabinet begged Nicholas to return to the capital and offered to resign completely. The Tsar, 800 kilometres (500&#160;mi) away, misinformed by the Minister of the Interior Alexander Protopopov that the situation was under control, ordered that firm steps be taken against the demonstrators. For this task, the Petrograd garrison was quite unsuitable. The cream of the old regular army had been destroyed in Poland and Galicia. In Petrograd, 170,000 recruits, country boys or older men from the working-class suburbs of the capital itself, were available under the command of officers at the front and cadets not yet graduated from the military academies. The units in the capital, although many bore the names of famous Imperial Guard regiments, were in reality rear or reserve battalions of these regiments, the regular units being away at the front. Many units, lacking both officers and rifles, had never undergone formal training.<sup id="cite_ref-tames53_125-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames53-125">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>General Khabalov attempted to put the Tsar's instructions into effect on the morning of Sunday, 11 March 1917. Despite huge posters ordering people to keep off the streets, vast crowds gathered and were only dispersed after some 200 had been shot dead, though a company of the <a href="/wiki/Volinsky_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="Volinsky Regiment">Volinsky Regiment</a> fired into the air rather than into the mob, and a company of the <a href="/wiki/Pavlovsky_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="Pavlovsky Regiment">Pavlovsky Life Guards</a> shot the officer who gave the command to open fire. Nicholas, informed of the situation by Rodzianko, ordered reinforcements to the capital and suspended the Duma.<sup id="cite_ref-tames53_125-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames53-125">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> However, it was too late.
</p><p>On 12 March, the Volinsky Regiment mutinied and was quickly followed by the <a href="/wiki/Semenovsky_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="Semenovsky Regiment">Semenovsky</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Izmaylovsky_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="Izmaylovsky Regiment">Ismailovsky</a>, the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Litovsky_Life_Guards&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Litovsky Life Guards (page does not exist)">Litovsky Life Guards</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9giment_de_la_Garde_Litovski" class="extiw" title="fr:Régiment de la Garde Litovski">fr</a>; <a href="https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%9B%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%BE%D0%B2%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%B9_%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%B9%D0%B1-%D0%B3%D0%B2%D0%B0%D1%80%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%B8_%D0%BF%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%BA" class="extiw" title="ru:Литовский лейб-гвардии полк">ru</a>&#93;</span> and even the legendary <a href="/wiki/Preobrazhensky_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="Preobrazhensky Regiment">Preobrazhensky Regiment</a> of the Imperial Guard, the oldest and staunchest regiment founded by <a href="/wiki/Peter_I_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter I of Russia">Peter the Great</a>. The arsenal was pillaged and the Ministry of the Interior, Military Government building, police headquarters, Law Courts and a score of police buildings were set on fire. By noon, the fortress of Peter and Paul, with its heavy artillery, was in the hands of the insurgents. By nightfall, 60,000 soldiers had joined the revolution.<sup id="cite_ref-tames53_125-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tames53-125">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Order broke down and Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Nikolai_Golitsyn" title="Nikolai Golitsyn">Nikolai Golitsyn</a> resigned; members of the Duma and the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_(council)#Russian_Revolution" title="Soviet (council)">Soviet</a> formed a <a href="/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government" title="Russian Provisional Government">Provisional Government</a> to try to restore order. They issued a demand that Nicholas must abdicate. Faced with this demand, which was echoed by his generals, deprived of loyal troops, with his family firmly in the hands of the Provisional Government, and fearful of unleashing civil war and opening the way for German conquest, Nicholas had little choice but to submit.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Revolution">Revolution</span></h2>
<h3><span id="Abdication_.281917.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Abdication_(1917)">Abdication (1917)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Abdication_of_Nicholas_II" title="Abdication of Nicholas II">Abdication of Nicholas II</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1134653256">.mw-parser-output .side-box{margin:4px 0;box-sizing:border-box;border:1px solid #aaa;font-size:88%;line-height:1.25em;background-color:#f9f9f9;display:flow-root}.mw-parser-output .side-box-abovebelow,.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{padding:0.25em 0.9em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-image{padding:2px 0 2px 0.9em;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-imageright{padding:2px 0.9em 2px 0;text-align:center}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .side-box-flex{display:flex;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{flex:1}}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .side-box{width:238px}.mw-parser-output .side-box-right{clear:right;float:right;margin-left:1em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-left{margin-right:1em}}</style><div class="side-box side-box-right plainlinks sistersitebox"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"/>
<div class="side-box-flex">
<div class="side-box-image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/38px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png" decoding="async" width="38" height="40" class="noviewer" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/57px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/76px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="410" data-file-height="430" /></div>
<div class="side-box-text plainlist"><a href="/wiki/Wikisource" title="Wikisource">Wikisource</a> has original text related to this article:
<div style="margin-left: 10px;"><b><a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Abdication_of_Nicholas_II" class="extiw" title="wikisource:Abdication of Nicholas II">Abdication of Nicholas II</a></b></div></div></div>
</div>
<p>Nicholas had suffered a <a href="/wiki/Coronary_occlusion" title="Coronary occlusion">coronary occlusion</a> only four days before his abdication.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> At the end of the "<a href="/wiki/February_Revolution" title="February Revolution">February Revolution</a>", Nicholas II chose to abdicate on 2 March (<a href="/wiki/Old_Style" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Style">O.S.</a>) / 15 March (<a href="/wiki/New_Style" class="mw-redirect" title="New Style">N.S.</a>) 1917. He first abdicated in favor of Alexei, but a few hours later changed his mind after advice from doctors that Alexei would not live long enough while separated from his parents, who would be forced into exile. Nicholas thus abdicated on behalf of his son, and drew up a new manifesto naming his brother, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Michael_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Michael Alexandrovich of Russia">Grand Duke Michael</a>, as the next Emperor of all the Russias. He issued a statement but it was suppressed by the Provisional Government. Michael declined to accept the throne until the people were allowed to vote through a Constituent Assembly for the continuance of the monarchy or a republic. The abdication of Nicholas II and Michael's deferment of accepting the throne brought three centuries of the Romanov dynasty's rule to an end. The fall of <a href="/wiki/Tsarist_autocracy" title="Tsarist autocracy">Tsarist autocracy</a> brought joy to liberals and socialists in Britain and France. The United States was the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government#World_recognition" title="Russian Provisional Government">first foreign government</a> to recognize the Provisional government. In Russia, the announcement of the Tsar's abdication was greeted with many emotions, including delight, relief, fear, anger and confusion.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Possibility_of_exile">Possibility of exile</span></h3>
<p>Both the Provisional Government and Nicholas wanted the royal family to go into exile following his abdication, with the United Kingdom being the preferred option.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> The British government reluctantly offered the family <a href="/wiki/Right_of_asylum" title="Right of asylum">asylum</a> on 19 March 1917, although it was suggested that it would be better for the Romanovs to go to a neutral country. News of the offer provoked uproar from the <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour Party</a> and many <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberals</a>, and the British ambassador Sir <a href="/wiki/George_Buchanan_(diplomat)" title="George Buchanan (diplomat)">George Buchanan</a> advised the government that the extreme left would use the ex-Tsar's presence "as an excuse for rousing public opinion against us".<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_Robert_K._1967_p._461_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_Robert_K._1967_p._461-129">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> The Liberal Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a> preferred that the family went to a neutral country, and wanted the offer to be announced as at the request of the Russian government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERappaport201862,_63_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERappaport201862,_63-130">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> The offer of asylum was withdrawn in April following objections by King <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">George V</a>, who, acting on the advice of his secretary <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Bigge,_1st_Baron_Stamfordham" title="Arthur Bigge, 1st Baron Stamfordham">Arthur Bigge, 1st Baron Stamfordham</a>, was worried that Nicholas's presence might provoke an uprising like the previous year's <a href="/wiki/Easter_Rising" title="Easter Rising">Easter Rising</a> in Ireland. However, later the king defied his secretary and went to the Romanov memorial service at the Russian Church in London.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelch2018246_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelch2018246-131">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> In the early summer of 1917, the Russian government approached the British government on the issue of asylum and was informed the offer had been withdrawn due to the considerations of British internal politics.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The French government declined to accept the Romanovs in view of increasing unrest on the Western Front and on the home front as a result of the ongoing war with Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> The British ambassador in Paris, Lord <a href="/wiki/Francis_Bertie" class="mw-redirect" title="Francis Bertie">Francis Bertie</a>, advised the Foreign Secretary that the Romanovs would be unwelcome in France as the ex-Empress was regarded as pro-German.<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_Robert_K._1967_p._461_129-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_Robert_K._1967_p._461-129">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Even if an offer of asylum had been forthcoming, there would have been other obstacles to be overcome. The Provisional Government only remained in power through an uneasy alliance with the <a href="/wiki/Petrograd_Soviet" title="Petrograd Soviet">Petrograd Soviet</a>, an arrangement known as "The <a href="/wiki/Dual_power" title="Dual power">Dual power</a>". An initial plan to send the royal family to the northern port of <a href="/wiki/Murmansk" title="Murmansk">Murmansk</a> had to be abandoned when it was realised that the railway workers and the soldiers guarding them were loyal to the Petrograd Soviet, which opposed the escape of the tsar; a later proposal to send the Romanovs to a neutral port in the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_Sea" title="Baltic Sea">Baltic Sea</a> via the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Finland" title="Grand Duchy of Finland">Grand Duchy of Finland</a> faced similar difficulties.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Imprisonment">Imprisonment</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nikolaus_II._(Russland).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Nikolaus_II._%28Russland%29.jpg/240px-Nikolaus_II._%28Russland%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="331" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Nikolaus_II._%28Russland%29.jpg/360px-Nikolaus_II._%28Russland%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Nikolaus_II._%28Russland%29.jpg/480px-Nikolaus_II._%28Russland%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1672" data-file-height="2308" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nikolaus_II._(Russland).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas II under guard in the grounds at <a href="/wiki/Tsarskoye_Selo" title="Tsarskoye Selo">Tsarskoye Selo</a> in the summer of 1917.</div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Tsarskoye_Selo">Tsarskoye Selo</span></h4>
<p>On 20 March 1917, the Provisional Government decreed that the royal family should be held under <a href="/wiki/House_arrest" title="House arrest">house arrest</a> in the Alexander Palace at Tsarskoye Selo. Nicholas joined the rest of the family there two days later, having traveled from the wartime headquarters at <a href="/wiki/Mogilev" title="Mogilev">Mogilev</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> The family had total privacy inside the palace, but walks in the grounds were strictly regulated.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Members of their domestic staff were allowed to stay if they wished and culinary standards were maintained.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Colonel <a href="/wiki/Eugene_Kobylinsky" title="Eugene Kobylinsky">Eugene Kobylinsky</a> was appointed to command the military garrison at Tsarskoye Selo,<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> which increasingly had to be done through negotiation with the committees or <a href="/wiki/Soviet_(council)" title="Soviet (council)">soviets</a> elected by the soldiers.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tobolskhouse2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Tobolskhouse2.jpg/240px-Tobolskhouse2.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="153" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Tobolskhouse2.jpg/360px-Tobolskhouse2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Tobolskhouse2.jpg/480px-Tobolskhouse2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1427" data-file-height="908" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Tobolskhouse2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Governor's Mansion in Tobolsk, where the Romanov family was held in captivity between August 1917 and April 1918</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:242px;"><a href="/wiki/File:AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg/240px-AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="178" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg/360px-AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg/480px-AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1368" data-file-height="1016" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:AlexeiNicholas1917.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas and Alexei sawing wood at Tobolsk in late 1917; a favourite pastime.</div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Tobolsk">Tobolsk</span></h4>
<p>That summer, the failure of the <a href="/wiki/Kerensky_Offensive" class="mw-redirect" title="Kerensky Offensive">Kerensky Offensive</a> against Austro-Hungarian and German forces in Galicia led to anti-government rioting in Petrograd, known as the <a href="/wiki/July_Days" title="July Days">July Days</a>. The government feared that further disturbances in the city could easily reach Tsarskoye Selo and it was decided to move the royal family to a safer location.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Kerensky" title="Alexander Kerensky">Alexander Kerensky</a>, who had taken over as prime minister, selected the town of <a href="/wiki/Tobolsk" title="Tobolsk">Tobolsk</a> in <a href="/wiki/Western_Siberia" title="Western Siberia">Western Siberia</a>, since it was remote from any large city and 150 miles (240&#160;km) from the nearest rail station.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Some sources state that there was an intention to send the family abroad in the spring of 1918 via Japan,<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> but more recent work suggests that this was just a Bolshevik rumour.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> The family left the Alexander Palace late on 13 August, reached <a href="/wiki/Tyumen" title="Tyumen">Tyumen</a> by rail four days later and then by two river ferries finally reached Tobolsk on 19 August.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> There they lived in the former <a href="/wiki/Governor%27s_Mansion_(Tobolsk,_Russia)" title="Governor&#39;s Mansion (Tobolsk, Russia)">Governor's Mansion</a> in considerable comfort. In October 1917, however, the <a href="/wiki/Bolsheviks" title="Bolsheviks">Bolsheviks</a> seized power from Kerensky's Provisional Government; Nicholas followed the events in October with interest but not yet with alarm. <a href="/wiki/Boris_Soloviev" class="mw-redirect" title="Boris Soloviev">Boris Soloviev</a>, the husband of <a href="/wiki/Maria_Rasputin" title="Maria Rasputin">Maria Rasputin</a>, attempted to organize a rescue with monarchical factions, but it came to nothing. Rumors persist that Soloviev was working for the Bolsheviks or the Germans, or both.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Separate preparations for a rescue by <a href="/wiki/Nikolai_Yevgenyevich_Markov" title="Nikolai Yevgenyevich Markov">Nikolai Yevgenyevich Markov</a> were frustrated by Soloviev's ineffectual activities.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas continued to underestimate <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin" title="Vladimir Lenin">Lenin</a>'s importance. In the meantime he and his family occupied themselves with reading books, exercising and playing games; Nicholas particularly enjoyed chopping firewood.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> However, in January 1918, the guard detachment's committee grew more assertive, restricting the hours that the family could spend in the grounds and banning them from walking to church on a Sunday as they had done since October.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> In a later incident, the soldiers tore the <a href="/wiki/Epaulette" title="Epaulette">epaulettes</a> from Kobylinsky's uniform, and he asked Nicholas not to wear his uniform outside for fear of provoking a similar event.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 1918, the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_People%27s_Commissars" title="Council of People&#39;s Commissars">Council of People's Commissars</a> (abbreviated to "Sovnarkom") in Moscow, the new capital, announced that the state subsidy for the family would be drastically reduced, starting on 1 March. This meant parting with twelve devoted servants and giving up butter and coffee as luxuries, even though Nicholas added to the funds from his own resources.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas and Alexandra were appalled by news of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Brest-Litovsk" title="Treaty of Brest-Litovsk">Treaty of Brest-Litovsk</a>, whereby Russia agreed to give up Poland, Finland, the Baltic States, most of Belarus, Ukraine, the Crimea, most of the Caucasus, and small parts of Russia proper including areas around <a href="/wiki/Pskov" title="Pskov">Pskov</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rostov-on-Don" title="Rostov-on-Don">Rostov-on-Don</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> What kept the family's spirits up was the belief that help was at hand.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> The Romanovs believed that various plots were underway to break them out of captivity and smuggle them to safety. The Western Allies lost interest in the fate of the Romanovs after Russia left the war. The German government wanted the monarchy restored in Russia to crush the Bolsheviks and maintain good relations with the Central Powers.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The situation in Tobolsk changed for the worse on 26 March, when 250 ill-disciplined <a href="/wiki/Red_Guards_(Russia)" title="Red Guards (Russia)">Red Guards</a> arrived from the regional capital, <a href="/wiki/Omsk" title="Omsk">Omsk</a>. Not to be outdone, the soviet in <a href="/wiki/Yekaterinburg" title="Yekaterinburg">Yekaterinburg</a>, the capital of the neighbouring <a href="/wiki/Ural_(region)" title="Ural (region)">Ural region</a>, sent 400 Red Guards to exert their influence on the town.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Disturbances between these rival groups and the lack of funds to pay the guard detachment caused them to send a delegation to Moscow to plead their case. The result was that Sovnarkom appointed their own <a href="/wiki/Commissar" title="Commissar">commissar</a> to take charge of Tobolsk and remove the Romanovs to Yekaterinburg, with the intention of eventually bringing Nicholas to a <a href="/wiki/Show_trial" title="Show trial">show trial</a> in Moscow.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> The man selected was <a href="/wiki/Vasily_Yakovlev" title="Vasily Yakovlev">Vasily Yakovlev</a>, a veteran Bolshevik,<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Recruiting a body of loyal men <i>en route</i>, Yakovlev arrived in Tobolsk on 22 April; he imposed his authority on the competing Red Guards factions, paid-off and <a href="/wiki/Demobilization" title="Demobilization">demobilized</a> the guard detachment, and placed further restrictions on the Romanovs.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> The next day, Yakovlev informed Kobylinsky that Nicholas was to be transferred to Yekaterinburg. Alexei was too ill to travel, so Alexandra elected to go with Nicholas along with Maria, while the other daughters would remain at Tobolsk until they were able to make the journey.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Yekaterinburg">Yekaterinburg</span></h4>
<p>At 3&#160;am on 25 April, the three Romanovs, their retinue, and the escort of Yakovlev's detachment, left Tobolsk in a convoy of nineteen <a href="/wiki/Tarantass" title="Tarantass">tarantasses</a> (four-wheeled carriages), as the river was still partly frozen which prevented the use of the ferry.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> After an arduous journey which included two overnight stops, fording rivers, frequent changes of horses and a foiled plot by the Yekaterinburg Red Guards to abduct and kill the prisoners, the party arrived at Tyumen and boarded a requisitioned train. Yakovlev was able to communicate securely with Moscow by means of a <a href="/wiki/David_Edward_Hughes" title="David Edward Hughes">Hughes</a>' <a href="/wiki/Teleprinter" title="Teleprinter">teleprinter</a> and obtained agreement to change their destination to Omsk, where it was thought that the leadership were less likely to harm the Romanovs.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Leaving Tyumen early on 28 April, the train left towards Yekaterinburg, but quickly changed direction towards Omsk. This led the Yekaterinburg leaders to believe that Yakovlev was a traitor who was trying to take Nicholas to exile by way of <a href="/wiki/Vladivostok" title="Vladivostok">Vladivostok</a>; telegraph messages were sent, two thousand armed men were mobilized and a train was dispatched to arrest Yakovlev and the Romanovs. The Romanovs' train was halted at Omsk station and after a frantic exchange of cables with Moscow, it was agreed that they should go to Yekaterinburg in return for a guarantee of safety for the royal family; they finally arrived there on the morning of 30 April.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>They were imprisoned in the two-story <a href="/wiki/Ipatiev_House" title="Ipatiev House">Ipatiev House</a>, the home of the military engineer Nikolay Nikolayevich Ipatiev, which ominously became referred to as the "house of special purpose". Here the Romanovs were kept under even stricter conditions; their retinue was further reduced and their possessions were searched.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Following allegations of pilfering from the royal household, <a href="/wiki/Yakov_Yurovsky" title="Yakov Yurovsky">Yakov Yurovsky</a>, a former member of the <a href="/wiki/Cheka" title="Cheka">Cheka</a> secret police, was appointed to command the guard detachment, a number of whom were replaced with trusted Latvian members of the Yekaterinburg "special-service detachment".<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> The remaining Romanovs left Tobolsk by river steamer on 20 May and arrived in Yekaterinburg three days later.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> By the first weeks of June, the Bolsheviks were becoming alarmed by the <a href="/wiki/Revolt_of_the_Czechoslovak_Legion" title="Revolt of the Czechoslovak Legion">Revolt of the Czechoslovak Legion</a>, whose forces were approaching the city from the east. This prompted a wave of executions and murders of those in the region who were believed to be <a href="/wiki/Counter-revolutionary" title="Counter-revolutionary">counter-revolutionaries</a>, including Grand Duke Michael, who was murdered in <a href="/wiki/Perm,_Russia" title="Perm, Russia">Perm</a> on 13 June.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although the Bolshevik leadership in Moscow still intended to bring Nicholas to trial, as the military situation deteriorated, <a href="/wiki/Leon_Trotsky" title="Leon Trotsky">Leon Trotsky</a> and <a href="/wiki/Yakov_Sverdlov" title="Yakov Sverdlov">Yakov Sverdlov</a> began to publicly equivocate about the possible fate of the former tsar.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> On 16 July, the Yekaterinburg leadership informed Yurovsky that it had been decided to execute the Romanovs as soon as approval arrived from Moscow, because the Czechs were expected to reach the city imminently. A coded telegram arrived in Moscow from Yekaterinburg that evening; after Lenin and Sverdlov had conferred a reply was sent, although no trace of that document has ever been found. In the meantime, Yurovsky had organized his firing squad and they waited through the night at the Ipatiev House for the signal to act.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Execution">Execution</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Execution_of_the_Romanov_family" class="mw-redirect" title="Execution of the Romanov family">Execution of the Romanov family</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg/220px-Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="195" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg/330px-Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg/440px-Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3711" data-file-height="3281" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Russian_Imperial_Family_1913.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nicholas with his family (left to right): Olga, Maria, Nicholas II, Alexandra Fyodorovna, Anastasia, Alexei, and Tatiana. <a href="/wiki/Livadia_Palace" title="Livadia Palace">Livadia Palace</a>, 1913.</div></div></div>
<p>There are several accounts of what happened and historians have not agreed on a solid, confirmed scope of events. According to the account of Bolshevik officer <a href="/wiki/Yakov_Yurovsky" title="Yakov Yurovsky">Yakov Yurovsky</a> (the chief executioner), in the early hours of 17 July 1918, the royal family was awakened around 2:00&#160;am, got dressed, and were led down into a half-basement room at the back of the Ipatiev house. The pretext for this move was the family's safety, i.e. that anti-Bolshevik forces were approaching Yekaterinburg, and the house might be fired upon.<sup id="cite_ref-Nicholas_1998_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nicholas_1998-169">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Present with Nicholas, Alexandra and their children were their doctor and three of their servants, who had voluntarily chosen to remain with the family: the Tsar's personal physician <a href="/wiki/Eugene_Botkin" title="Eugene Botkin">Eugene Botkin</a>, his wife's maid <a href="/wiki/Anna_Demidova" title="Anna Demidova">Anna Demidova</a>, and the family's chef, <a href="/wiki/Ivan_Kharitonov" title="Ivan Kharitonov">Ivan Kharitonov</a>, and footman, <a href="/wiki/Alexei_Trupp" title="Alexei Trupp">Alexei Trupp</a>. A firing squad had been assembled and was waiting in an adjoining room, composed of seven Communist soldiers from Central Europe, and three local Bolsheviks, all under the command of Yurovsky.<sup id="cite_ref-Nicholas_1998_169-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nicholas_1998-169">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nicholas was carrying his son. When the family arrived in the basement, the former Tzar asked if chairs could be brought in for his wife and son to sit on. Yurovsky ordered two chairs brought in, and when the empress and the heir were seated, the executioners filed into the room. Yurovsky announced to them that the Ural Soviet of Workers' Deputies had decided to execute them. A stunned Nicholas asked, "What? What did you say?" and turned toward his family. Yurovsky quickly repeated the order and Nicholas said, according to <a href="/wiki/Peter_Ermakov" title="Peter Ermakov">Peter Ermakov</a>, "You know not what you do."
</p><p>The executioners drew handguns and began shooting; Nicholas was the first to die. Yurovsky took credit afterwards for firing the first shot that killed the Tsar, but his protege – <a href="/wiki/Grigory_Petrovich_Nikulin" title="Grigory Petrovich Nikulin">Grigory Nikulin</a> – said years later that Mikhail Medvedev had fired the shot that killed Nicholas. "He fired the first shot. He killed the Tsar," he said in 1964 in a tape-recorded statement for the radio.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas was shot several times in the chest (sometimes erroneously said to have been shot in his head, but his skull bore no bullet wounds when it was discovered in 1991). Anastasia, Tatiana, Olga, and Maria survived the first hail of bullets; the sisters were wearing over 1.3 kilograms of diamonds and precious gems sewn into their clothing, which provided some initial protection from the bullets and bayonets.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> They were then stabbed with bayonets and finally shot at close range in their heads.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>An announcement from the Presidium of the Ural Regional Soviet of the Workers' and Peasants' Government emphasized that conspiracies had been exposed to free the ex-tsar, that counter-revolutionary forces were pressing in on Soviet Russian territory, and that the ex-tsar was guilty of unforgivable crimes against the nation.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>In view of the enemy's proximity to Yekaterinburg and the exposure by the Cheka of a serious White Guard plot with the goal of abducting the former Tsar and his family… In light of the approach of counterrevolutionary bands toward the Red capital of the Urals and the possibility of the crowned executioner escaping trial by the people (a plot among the White Guards to try to abduct him and his family was exposed and the compromising documents will be published), the Presidium of the Ural Regional Soviet, fulfilling the will of the Revolution, resolved to shoot the former Tsar, Nikolai Romanov, who is guilty of countless, bloody, violent acts against the Russian people.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The bodies were driven to nearby woodland, searched and burned. The remains were soaked in acid and finally thrown down a disused <a href="/wiki/Mineshaft" class="mw-redirect" title="Mineshaft">mineshaft</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> On the following day, other members of the Romanov family including <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Elizabeth_Feodorovna_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Duchess Elizabeth Feodorovna of Russia">Grand Duchess Elizabeth Feodorovna</a>, the empress's sister, who were being held in a school at <a href="/wiki/Alapayevsk" title="Alapayevsk">Alapayevsk</a>, were taken to another mine shaft and thrown in alive, except for <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Sergei_Mikhailovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Sergei Mikhailovich of Russia">Grand Duke Sergei Mikhailovich</a> who was shot when he tried to resist.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Identification">Identification</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg/220px-Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg/330px-Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg/440px-Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1382" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ipatjew-Haus2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Ipatiev House, Yekaterinburg, (later Sverdlovsk) in 1928</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:ChurchOn_Blood.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/ChurchOn_Blood.jpg/220px-ChurchOn_Blood.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/ChurchOn_Blood.jpg/330px-ChurchOn_Blood.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/ChurchOn_Blood.jpg/440px-ChurchOn_Blood.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:ChurchOn_Blood.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Yekaterinburg's "<a href="/wiki/All_Saints_Resplendent_in_the_Russian_Land" class="mw-redirect" title="All Saints Resplendent in the Russian Land">Church on the Blood</a>", built on the spot where the Ipatiev House once stood</div></div></div>
<p>In 1979, the bodies of Tsar Nicholas II, Tsaritsa Alexandra, three of their daughters, and those of four non-family members killed with them, were discovered near <a href="/wiki/Yekaterinburg" title="Yekaterinburg">Sverdlovsk</a> (Yekaterinburg) by amateur archaeologist <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Avdonin" title="Alexander Avdonin">Alexander Avdonin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Massie2012_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie2012-177">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> In January 1998, the remains excavated from underneath the dirt road near Yekaterinburg were officially identified as those of Nicholas II and his family, excluding one daughter (either Maria or Anastasia) and Alexei. The identifications—including comparisons to a living relative, performed by separate Russian, British and American scientists using <a href="/wiki/DNA_analysis" class="mw-redirect" title="DNA analysis">DNA analysis</a>—concur and were found to be conclusive.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 2007, an amateur historian discovered bones near Yekaterinburg belonging to a boy and young woman.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
Prosecutors reopened the investigation into the deaths of the imperial family<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> and, in April 2008, DNA tests performed by an American laboratory proved that bone fragments exhumed in the Ural Mountains belonged to two children of Nicholas II, Alexei and a daughter.<sup id="cite_ref-AP_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AP-185">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> That same day it was announced by Russian authorities that remains from the entire family had been recovered.<sup id="cite_ref-AP_185-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AP-185">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 1 October 2008, the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Russia" title="Supreme Court of Russia">Supreme Court of Russia</a> ruled that Nicholas II and his family were victims of political persecution and should be rehabilitated.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> In March 2009, results of the DNA testing were published, confirming that the two bodies discovered in 2007 were those of Alexei and one of his sisters.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In late 2015, at the insistence of the Russian Orthodox Church,<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Russian investigators exhumed the bodies of Nicholas II and his wife, Alexandra, for additional DNA testing,<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> which confirmed that the bones were of the couple.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Funeral">Funeral</span></h2>
<p>After the DNA testing of 1998, the remains of the Emperor and his immediate family were interred at <a href="/wiki/Saints_Peter_and_Paul_Cathedral,_Saint_Petersburg" title="Saints Peter and Paul Cathedral, Saint Petersburg">St. Peter and Paul Cathedral</a>, Saint Petersburg, on 17 July 1998, on the eightieth anniversary of their assassination. The ceremony was attended by Russian President <a href="/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin" title="Boris Yeltsin">Boris Yeltsin</a>, who said, "Today is a historic day for Russia. For many years, we kept quiet about this monstrous crime, but the truth has to be spoken."<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British Royal Family was represented at the funeral by <a href="/wiki/Prince_Michael_of_Kent" title="Prince Michael of Kent">Prince Michael of Kent</a>, and more than twenty ambassadors to Russia, including <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Wood_(diplomat)" title="Andrew Wood (diplomat)">Sir Andrew Wood</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Bukovsky" title="John Bukovsky">Archbishop John Bukovsky</a>, and <a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst-J%C3%B6rg_von_Studnitz" class="extiw" title="de:Ernst-Jörg von Studnitz">Ernst-Jörg von Studnitz</a>, were also in attendance.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sainthood">Sainthood</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Canonization_of_the_Romanovs" title="Canonization of the Romanovs">Canonization of the Romanovs</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tsarebozhiye" title="Tsarebozhiye">Tsarebozhiye</a></div>
<p>In 1981, Nicholas and his immediate family were <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">recognised</a> as <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyred</a> <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saints</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_of_Russia" title="Russian Orthodox Church Outside of Russia">Russian Orthodox Church Outside of Russia</a>. On 14 August 2000, they were recognised by the <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a>. This time they were not named as martyrs, since their deaths did not result immediately from their Christian faith; instead, they were canonized as <a href="/wiki/Passion_bearer" title="Passion bearer">passion bearers</a>. According to a statement by the Moscow synod, they were glorified as saints for the following reasons:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>In the last Orthodox Russian monarch and members of his family we see people who sincerely strove to incarnate in their lives the commands of the Gospel. In the suffering borne by the Royal Family in prison with humility, patience, and meekness, and in their martyrs' deaths in Yekaterinburg in the night of 17 July 1918 was revealed the light of the faith of Christ that conquers evil.</p></blockquote>
<p>However, Nicholas' canonization was controversial. The Russian Orthodox Church Abroad was split on the issue back in 1981, some members suggesting that the emperor was a weak ruler and had failed to thwart the rise of the Bolsheviks. It was pointed out by one priest that martyrdom in the Russian Orthodox Church has nothing to do with the martyr's personal actions but is instead related to why he or she was killed.<sup id="cite_ref-Massie_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie-197">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Russian Orthodox Church inside Russia rejected the family's classification as martyrs because they were not killed on account of their religious faith. Religious leaders in both churches also had objections to canonising the Tsar's family because they perceived him as a weak emperor whose incompetence led to the revolution and the suffering of his people and made him partially responsible for his own assassination and those of his wife, children and servants. For these opponents, the fact that the Tsar was, in private life, a kind man and a good husband and father or a leader who showed genuine concern for the peasantry did not override his poor governance of Russia.<sup id="cite_ref-Massie_197-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie-197">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite the original opposition, the Russian Orthodox Church inside Russia ultimately recognised the family as "passion bearers", or people who met their deaths with Christian humility. Since the late 20th century, believers have attributed healing from illnesses or conversion to the Orthodox Church to their prayers to the children of Nicholas, Maria and Alexei, as well as to the rest of the family.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<p>Contemporary evaluations of Nicholas portrayed him as a well-meaning but indecisive leader, whose actions as monarch were heavily influenced by his advisors. Historian Raymond Esthus states:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The contemporary assessments of Nicholas are remarkably uniform. He was described as shy, charming, gentle in disposition, fearful of controversy, indecisive, indulgent to his relatives, and deeply devoted to his family. Aleksandr Mosolov, who headed his Court Chancellery for sixteen years, wrote that Nicholas, though intelligent and well-educated, never adopted a definite, energetic attitude and loathed making a decision in the presence of others. Sergei Witte, who served Nicholas and his father for eleven years as Minister of Finance, commented that the Tsar was a well-intentioned child, but his actions were entirely dependent upon the character of his counselors, most of whom were bad.<sup id="cite_ref-Esthus,_Raymond_A._1981_396–411_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Esthus,_Raymond_A._1981_396–411-19">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet period</a>, Nicholas II's legacy was widely criticised within Russia, although discussion was heavily influenced by <a href="/wiki/Propaganda_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Propaganda in the Soviet Union">state propaganda</a>, which described him as a bloodthirsty tyrant. Pavel Bykov, who wrote the first full account of the downfall of the Tsar for the new Soviet government, denounced Nicholas as a "tyrant, who paid with his life for the age-old repression and arbitrary rule of his ancestors over the Russian people, over the impoverished and blood-soaked country". Soviet-era historians described Nicholas II as unfit for rule, arguing that he had a weak will and was manipulated by adventurist forces. He was also criticised for fanning nationalism and chauvinism, and his regime was condemned for its extensive use of the army, police, and courts to destroy the revolutionary movement. During his reign, Nicholas had become known as "Nicholas the Bloody" for his role in the Khodynka Tragedy and the suppression of the 1905 Revolution.<sup id="cite_ref-Kallistov_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kallistov-17">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For most of the 20th century, Nicholas was generally considered by historians to have been incompetent at the colossal task of ruling the enormous Russian Empire, although the influence of Soviet propaganda on general opinion must be considered.<sup id="cite_ref-Ferro,_Marc_1995_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ferro,_Marc_1995-20">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Barbara_Tuchman" class="mw-redirect" title="Barbara Tuchman">Barbara Tuchman</a> provides a damning evaluation of his reign in her 1962 book <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guns_of_August" title="The Guns of August">The Guns of August</a></i>, describing his sole focus as sovereign as being "to preserve intact the absolute monarchy bequeathed to him by his father", and writing that, "lacking the intellect, energy or training for his job", Nicholas "fell back on personal favorites, whim, simple mulishness, and other devices of the empty-headed autocrat ... when a telegram was brought to him announcing the annihilation of the Russian fleet at Tsushima, he read it, stuffed it in his pocket, and went on playing tennis."<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historian <a href="/wiki/Robert_K._Massie" title="Robert K. Massie">Robert K. Massie</a> provides a similar indictment of his incompetence, although he emphasises Nicholas' personal morality, describing him as a tragic figure:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p> ... there still are those who for political or other reasons continue to insist that Nicholas was "Bloody Nicholas". Most commonly, he is described as shallow, weak, stupid—a one-dimensional figure presiding feebly over the last days of a corrupt and crumbling system. This, certainly, is the prevailing public image of the last Tsar. Historians admit that Nicholas was a "good man"—the historical evidence of personal charm, gentleness, love of family, deep religious faith and strong Russian patriotism is too overwhelming to be denied—but they argue that personal factors are irrelevant; what matters is that Nicholas was a bad tsar&#160;.... Essentially, the tragedy of Nicholas II was that he appeared in the wrong place in history.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Following the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Dissolution of the Soviet Union">collapse of the Soviet Union</a>, present-day Russian historians give Nicholas a more positive assessment, particularly when evaluating the reforms made by the Russian state during his reign.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Titles.2C_styles.2C_honours_and_arms"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Titles,_styles,_honours_and_arms">Titles, styles, honours and arms</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-weight: normal; background-color: #CCBBEE; color: #000066;"><span style="font-size:80%">Styles of</span><br /><b><span class="fn">Nicholas II of Russia</span></b></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Russian_Empire.svg" class="image"><img alt="Coat of Arms of Russian Empire.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Coat_of_Arms_of_Russian_Empire.svg/75px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Russian_Empire.svg.png" decoding="async" width="75" height="91" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Coat_of_Arms_of_Russian_Empire.svg/113px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Russian_Empire.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Coat_of_Arms_of_Russian_Empire.svg/150px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Russian_Empire.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="505" data-file-height="612" /></a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Style_(manner_of_address)" class="mw-redirect" title="Style (manner of address)">Reference style</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/His_Imperial_Majesty" class="mw-redirect" title="His Imperial Majesty">His Imperial Majesty</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spoken style</th><td class="infobox-data">Your Imperial Majesty</td></tr></tbody></table>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Titles_and_styles">Titles and styles</span></h3>
<p>Nicholas II's full title as Emperor, as set forth in Article 59 of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Constitution_of_1906" title="Russian Constitution of 1906">1906 Constitution</a>, was: "By the Grace of God, We Nicholas, Emperor and Autocrat of <a href="/wiki/All_the_Russias" class="mw-redirect" title="All the Russias">All the Russias</a>, of Moscow, <a href="/wiki/Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiev">Kiev</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vladimir-Suzdal" title="Vladimir-Suzdal">Vladimir</a>, <a href="/wiki/Novgorod" class="mw-redirect" title="Novgorod">Novgorod</a>; Tsar of <a href="/wiki/Kazan" title="Kazan">Kazan</a>, Tsar of <a href="/wiki/Astrakhan" title="Astrakhan">Astrakhan</a>, Tsar of <a href="/wiki/Congress_Poland" title="Congress Poland">Poland</a>, Tsar of <a href="/wiki/Siberia" title="Siberia">Siberia</a>, Tsar of <a href="/wiki/Tauric_Chersonesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Tauric Chersonesus">Tauric Chersonesus</a>, Tsar of <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(country)" title="Georgia (country)">Georgia</a>; Lord of <a href="/wiki/Pskov" title="Pskov">Pskov</a>, and Grand Prince of <a href="/wiki/Smolensk" title="Smolensk">Smolensk</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lithuania" title="Lithuania">Lithuania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Volhynia" title="Volhynia">Volhynia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Podolia" title="Podolia">Podolia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Finland" title="Finland">Finland</a>; Prince of <a href="/wiki/Estonia" title="Estonia">Estonia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Livonia" title="Livonia">Livonia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Courland" title="Courland">Courland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Semigalia" class="mw-redirect" title="Semigalia">Semigalia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Samogitia" title="Samogitia">Samogitia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bia%C5%82ystok" title="Białystok">Bielostok</a>, <a href="/wiki/Karelia" title="Karelia">Karelia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tver" title="Tver">Tver</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yugor" class="mw-redirect" title="Yugor">Yugor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Perm,_Russia" title="Perm, Russia">Perm</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kirov,_Kirov_Oblast" title="Kirov, Kirov Oblast">Vyatka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bogar" title="Bogar">Bogar</a> and others; Sovereign and Grand Prince of <a href="/wiki/Nizhni_Novgorod" class="mw-redirect" title="Nizhni Novgorod">Nizhni Novgorod</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chernigov" class="mw-redirect" title="Chernigov">Chernigov</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ryazan" title="Ryazan">Ryazan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Polotsk" title="Polotsk">Polotsk</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rostov" title="Rostov">Rostov</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jaroslavl" class="mw-redirect" title="Jaroslavl">Jaroslavl</a>, <a href="/wiki/Beloozero" class="mw-redirect" title="Beloozero">Beloozero</a>, <a href="/wiki/Udoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Udoria">Udoria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Obdoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Obdoria">Obdoria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kondia" title="Kondia">Kondia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vitebsk" title="Vitebsk">Vitebsk</a>, Mstislav, and Ruler of all the <a href="/wiki/Severia" title="Severia">Severian</a> country; Sovereign and Lord of <a href="/wiki/Caucasian_Iberia" class="mw-redirect" title="Caucasian Iberia">Iveria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kartli" title="Kartli">Kartalinia</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Kabard" class="mw-redirect" title="Kabard">Kabardian</a> lands and Armenian province: hereditary Sovereign and Possessor of the <a href="/wiki/Circassia" title="Circassia">Circassian</a> and Mountain Princes and of others; Sovereign of <a href="/wiki/Turkestan" title="Turkestan">Turkestan</a>, Heir of <a href="/wiki/Norway" title="Norway">Norway</a>, Duke of <a href="/wiki/Schleswig-Holstein" title="Schleswig-Holstein">Schleswig-Holstein</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stormarn_(gau)" title="Stormarn (gau)">Stormarn</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dithmarschen" title="Dithmarschen">Dithmarschen</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Oldenburg" title="Duchy of Oldenburg">Oldenburg</a>, and so forth, and so forth, and so forth."<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:282px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg/280px-Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="195" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg/420px-Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg/560px-Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1328" data-file-height="925" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land_Russian_Empire_Map.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Nicholas II Land">Emperor Nicholas II Land</a> in a 1915 map of the Russian Empire. At the time it was believed that what is now Severnaya Zemlya was a single landmass.</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Emperor_Nicholas_II_Land" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Nicholas II Land">Emperor Nicholas II Land</a> (Russian: <span lang="ru">Земля Императора Николая II</span>, <span title="Russian-language text"><i lang="ru-Latn">Zemlya Imperatora Nikolaya II</i></span>) was discovered in 1913 by the <a href="/wiki/Arctic_Ocean_Hydrographic_Expedition" title="Arctic Ocean Hydrographic Expedition">Arctic Ocean Hydrographic Expedition</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Boris_Vilkitsky" title="Boris Vilkitsky">Boris Vilkitsky</a> on behalf of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Hydrographic_Service" title="Russian Hydrographic Service">Russian Hydrographic Service</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Barr1975_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barr1975-205">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Still incompletely surveyed, the new territory was officially named in the Emperor's honour by order of the Secretary of the Imperial Navy in 1914.<sup id="cite_ref-arkhipelag_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-arkhipelag-206">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> The archipelago was renamed "<a href="/wiki/Severnaya_Zemlya" title="Severnaya Zemlya">Severnaya Zemlya</a>" in 1926 by the <a href="/wiki/Presidium" title="Presidium">Presidium</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Central_Executive_Committee_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Central Executive Committee of the Soviet Union">Central Executive Committee of the Soviet Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-newslab_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-newslab-207">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li><span class="datasortkey" data-sort-value="United Kingdom"><span class="flagicon"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="thumbborder" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/46px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="600" />&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">United Kingdom</a></span>:
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Stranger Knight of the Garter</a>, <i>1 July 1893</i><sup id="cite_ref-p69_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p69-232">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup></li>

<li><a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Bath" title="Order of the Bath">Honorary Grand Cross of the Bath</a> (military), <i>20 October 1916</i><sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></li></ul></div>
<p>Nicholas II was granted honorary senior rank in a number of foreign armies, reciprocating by extending similar distinctions to a number of his fellow monarchs. These included the Imperial German, Spanish, Italian, Danish and British armies.
</p><p>He was <a href="/wiki/Colonel-in-Chief" class="mw-redirect" title="Colonel-in-Chief">Colonel-in-Chief</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Scots_Greys" title="Royal Scots Greys">Royal Scots Greys</a> from 1894 until his death. On becoming Colonel-in-Chief he presented the Regiment with a white <a href="/wiki/Bearskin" title="Bearskin">bearskin</a>, now worn by the bass drummer of the Pipes and Drums of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Scots_Dragoon_Guards" title="Royal Scots Dragoon Guards">Royal Scots Dragoon Guards</a>. The Imperial Russian anthem is still played at dinner nights in the Officers' Mess, where there remains a portrait of the Tsar in Scots Greys uniform. Since his death, the Regiment has worn a black backing behind its cap badge as a symbol of <a href="/wiki/Mourning" title="Mourning">mourning</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arms">Arms</span></h3>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Wealth">Wealth</span></h2>
<p>Estimates of Nicholas II's personal wealth have been vastly exaggerated. As <i>Emperor of All The Russias</i>, and an <a href="/wiki/Autocracy" title="Autocracy">autocrat</a>, the resources under his command were virtually incalculable. However, the vast majority of this was owned by the state as <a href="/wiki/Crown_property" class="mw-redirect" title="Crown property">Crown property</a>; the Romanov family's <i>personal wealth</i> was only a small fraction of this. As monarch, the income of Nicholas was 24 million gold <a href="/wiki/Ruble" title="Ruble">roubles</a> per annum: this derived from a yearly allowance from the Treasury, and from the profits of Crown farmland.<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_Robert_K._1967,_p._64_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_Robert_K._1967,_p._64-240">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> From this income, he had to fund staff, the upkeep of imperial palaces and imperial theatres, annuities for the royal family, pensions, bequests, and other outgoings. "Before the end of the year, the Tsar was usually penniless; sometimes he reached this embarrassing state by autumn."<sup id="cite_ref-Massie,_Robert_K._1967,_p._64_240-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Massie,_Robert_K._1967,_p._64-240">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> According to the Grand Marshal of the Court, Count Paul Benckendorff, the family's total financial resources amounted to between 12.5 and 17.5 million roubles.<sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> As a comparison, Prince Felix Yusupov estimated his family's worth in real estate holdings alone as amounting to 50 million gold roubles.<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Documentaries_and_films">Documentaries and films</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_films_about_the_Romanovs" title="List of films about the Romanovs">List of films about the Romanovs</a></div>
<p>Several films about Nicholas II and his family have been made, including <i><a href="/wiki/Anastasia_(1956_film)" title="Anastasia (1956 film)">Anastasia</a></i> (1956), <i><a href="/wiki/Nicholas_and_Alexandra" title="Nicholas and Alexandra">Nicholas and Alexandra</a></i> (1971), <i><a href="/wiki/Anastasia:_The_Mystery_of_Anna" title="Anastasia: The Mystery of Anna">Anastasia: The Mystery of Anna</a></i> (1986), <i><a href="/wiki/Rasputin:_Dark_Servant_of_Destiny" title="Rasputin: Dark Servant of Destiny">Rasputin: Dark Servant of Destiny</a></i> (1996 <a href="/wiki/HBO" title="HBO">HBO</a>), <i><a href="/wiki/Anastasia_(1997_film)" title="Anastasia (1997 film)">Anastasia</a></i> (1997), and two Russian adaptations <i><a href="/wiki/Assassin_of_the_Tsar" class="mw-redirect" title="Assassin of the Tsar">Assassin of the Tsar</a></i> (1991) and <i><a href="/wiki/The_Romanovs:_An_Imperial_Family" title="The Romanovs: An Imperial Family">The Romanovs: An Imperial Family</a></i> (2000).
In 2017 the film <i><a href="/wiki/Matilda_(2017_film)" title="Matilda (2017 film)">Matilda</a></i> was released. <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Czars" title="The Last Czars">The Last Czars</a></i> was released by Netflix in 2019.
<i>God Save Russia</i> – documentary film by <a href="/wiki/W%C5%82odzimierz_Szpak" title="Włodzimierz Szpak">Włodzimierz Szpak</a> (1990).
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span></h3>
<ul><li><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1041539562">.mw-parser-output .citation{word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}</style><span class="citation wikicite" id="endnote_dates"><b><a href="#ref_dates">^O.S./N.S.</a></b></span> Over the course of Nicholas's life, two calendars were used: the <a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">Old Style</a> <a href="/wiki/Julian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian Calendar">Julian Calendar</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">New Style</a> <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregorian Calendar">Gregorian Calendar</a>. Russia switched from the Julian to the Gregorian calendar on 1 February (O.S.) / 14 February (N.S.) 1918.</li></ul>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
